FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

In spite of the constant revolutions of the wheel of fashion, the coat and skirt costume remains a necessary item of the .outfit for the summer holidays. Nowadays things have changed so much, and there are so many cheap trips to the seaside, that we need very many more clothes than formerly. Indeed, it is truly amazing how many frocks are abso-

Iluteiy necessary in order to be suitably dressed on all occasions. First and foremost in my list of necessaries I place the coat and ci skirt costume, which should preferably be of li blue serge, and with the skirt cut so as to

ead15,,1fth.7.111 I

A SMART TOILETTE:

clear the ground all round. I should suggest that a bright shade of navy blue is the colour selected, and that the serge is of the non- shrinkable kind. The skirt would look best knife-pleated, but some people find this variety of the kilted skirt heavy to wear, in which case the skirt should fit the figure closely to the knees and then spring out fully, the insertion of a few fan pleats help- ing to produce this effects There is also the skirt, which is arranged in simulated over- skirts arranged on a foundation of silk. Braidings are also greatly in vogue, and go very well with serge, whether white, red, or black braid is employed. One very effective seaside toilette of navy blue serge I recently admired was trimmed with black silk braid, arranged over white silk braid, the white silk showing through the pattern of the .braidings. The smart toilette depicted is however guiltless of any sort of braiding or trimming, being pleated both on the . skirt and short coat, and with only large flat gilt buttons as a relief, The coats this season -are all short, and mostly loose in shape; so popular indeed has the short sac or loose bolero become, that a whisper is already getting abroad that its reign is fast drawing to a close, and that the early winter modes will evince a marked leaning towards the tight-fitting coat, which really shows a good figure off to the best advantage,. A tightly- clinging coat is also warmer for winter wear,

A NEAT COATJ

while the loose coat is cooler for summer wear, and will remain in vogue till the days of summer are a thing of the past. These little coatees are made with either a wide collar of silk or lace, or both intermingled, or are altogether collarless, having only a little band of the material itself stitched in lines, or else of some sort of handsome embroidery., The newest coats do not as a rule show a collar of. lace only, but the lace is used in the form of motifs laid on the silk, which is either plain or pleated:. _

The neat little coat sketched was worn with a brown voile skirt and in it the lace motifs are arranged on the pleated silk. The skirt which was worn with this was one of these exceedingly pretty eight-gored short skirts cut with a very pronounced flare at the feet, and strapped at the hips and near the edge of the flounce. The advantage of all the gores and stitchings on the skirt is that they all help to keep it in place and to make it straight and flat, while preventing it from bulging at the knees. These are all very good qualities, as the up-to-date skirt i•-; not always to be depended on by reason of its tendency to elaborations One girl of my acquaintance who requires to study economy has hit upon the ingenious plan of having two skirts to one coat, thereby saving room in the arrangement of the contents of her trilnks when visiting. SIMPLE HAT.

made simply and cut rather snort, while the other is more lengthy and likewise more elaborate. One can always smarten up a coat by the addition of a handsome lace collar, a dainty neck ruffle, or a fluffy feather boa; and the coat should always be made to wear either open or closed—in the former case the style of blouse visible underneath emphasising or lessening the dressiness of the tout ensemble.

The simple hat sketched is of light burnt straw trimmed with folds of foulard- ribbon brought over one side of the brim and made into a soft bow with fringed ends falling on the hair. This style of hat is much in demand for seaside and country Wear. A very pretty afternoon or promermde toilette I saw in wear at one of the smartest seaside resorts on our coasts was of thin black material covered with s.mall white spots and trimmed- with white lace. WOMEN'S GHAT.

The year that has passed since the Coronation has been a very busy one for the King, and the activity which his Majesty has shewn in the discharge of so many public functions is evidence of the excellent health which the Sovereign', enjoys. One sometimes hears the remark that the King is one of the hardest worked men, but, however that may be, his Majesty is certainly one of the greatest travellers and there is scarcely any part of the country that the Sovereign has not visited dnring the past year. The most notable visits were those to Scotland and Ireland, and the year also includes visits to the Scilly Isles, the Isle of Man, and various other parts of the Kingdom. Then his Majesty's trip to the Continent included visits to the King of Portugal, the King •of Italy, and President Loubet, and among other noteworthy events of the period may be mentioned the visits to this country of the German Emperor, the King of Tortuga', and the President of the French Republic. One of the quarterlies discusses the tradition that the Barons King sale have the right to remain covered in the presence of the Sovereign. The discussion reminds one of a remark from an old resident in a provincial town, who was asked why he did not go to see London. "I do not believ," he said "that there is a better place than—, and if there were, I don't see what any- body could want with it." It does not seem to matter very much whether the lords of Kingsale have this right or not. Probably the story is all a myth; but if it were not, the right is scarcely one that anybody would wish to exercise, and if that is so, he might as well be without it.

This is the time of year when the country visitor appears in London, and if it were not for the policeman, the stranger would often Wrat a loss for information upon points which are of everyday concern to him. There is for instance, the difficulty of discovering the right busses and trains, which are mostly without any clear or regular system of indicating where they go to. Even the long resident Londoner has the greatest diffi- culty in ferreting out the right 'bus, and it must bestill more of a trouble to the stranger. Another, and equally unnecessary source of difficulty is, that thetheatres still omit to advertise their addree.s. When the play going public was small, and the number of theatres few, this information may have been superfluous, but now that the theatres are scattered in all directions -an intithation of their precise locality would save manypeople a good deal of trouble.

Tl-e house boat is a familiar object at many of our river resorts, and it is being more and more used by holiday makers on several of the Scottish Lochs. Many of these. such as the famous Loch Lomond with its finely wooded shores, and beautiful little islands, afford ideal surroundings for a holiday on a houseboat, though no doubt many Scotsmen will regard the luxuriantly appointed houseboat as in strange contrast to the old fashioned Scottish Way of enjoying mountain air and scenery. But luxury always follows hard upon the beaten track of the fashionable tourist and no doubt before long gorgeously appointed houseboats will be as common on the Scottish lochs as they are at Henley. Excellent fishing is nearly always to be had on the lochs, and for the angler there could hardly be a better holiday than one on a houseboat in the beautiful Scottish Highlands. Though women are generally timid of small or imaginary dangers, they are often as reckless as the most foolhardy men in exposing themselves to hazardous risks in mountain climbing, boating, bathing, and other fruitful causes of holiday accidents. One is reminded of this by the plight of the two ladies who in spite of all warnings would attemptitheascent of one of the Grison mountains, though it was known to be unsafe owing to snowfalls, and heavy rains. They were fortunate enough to be rescued in time, but it is foolish adventures of this kind that give rise to so many accidents during the holiday season. The lonely flower on the edge of the cliff, or the sprig of edelweiss hanging ever the abyss, seem to have an irresistible attraction for adventurous spirits and no warnings are sufficient to prevent them risking their lives in attempting foolish and hazardous feats.

The re-appearimce of the small waist is a matter to be deplored. That women and girls owe much of their present fine appearance and splendid health to the loose, straight-fronted corset, is an indisputable fact, and we can only regret that the craze for thehideous unnatural, wasp-like waist, should have been started again. The day when we all, like sheep, followed one another through the whims and fancies of Dame Fashion, are past, and though there are many who will follow the few smart leaders in this mode, as in all others, there are happily vast numbers of -women who prefer athletics and good health, to a red nose and the divers serious ills to which tight lacing so often leads. Tweeds and friezes of home manufacture are being much more worn this holiday season than was the case last year. Then zibeline carried all before it. Light in weight, and warm with their almost waterproof resist- ance to rain, and hardly showing dust or mud at all,they are ideal fabrics for travelling and knock-about gowns. Moreover one has the very comfortableifeeling that one is helping the home industries, saving one's pocket, and presenting a good appearance, when garbed in these particular materials. Bags and satchels are in as great request as ever, and this state of affairs will continue while pockets remain forbidden luxuries. For hard wear, the leather wallet, slung at the side by a chatelaine hook, is the most useful, then for more dressy occasions come the suede and antelope bags, while the bags of silk encased in a lattice-work of gold and silver, studded with spark- ling jewels, are luxuries only possible to the well- endowed with this world's goods. To the light-fingered individual, these dainty costly items must boa tempta- tion indeed, and it is surprising that we do not hear of more thefts of this description. The mantle of the late Miss Omerod has fallen upon another lady, Miss Alice L. Embleton, who has taken up the study of insect plagues, which cause so much trouble to farmers, gardeners and fruit growers. Miss Embleton was the first lady to be admitted a member of the Linnacan Society,and she has already achieved some measure of success, in dealing with small scale, that little pest which is so destructive to palms and ferns. Miss Etnbleton's method is the cultivation of parasites that prey upon the parasites which destroy vegetation. All insects, we are told, have their parasites, which in turn have others, and so on ad infinitum. It is by the study of these that Miss Embleton hopes to discover the most effectual insecticides for the pests which cause so much loss to farmers, horticulturists, and market gardeners. MADGE.

A WALKING BANK.

Did you ever hear of a living, walking bank? Did you ever see one? The answer must be in the affirma- tive. Every living man and woman is really a bank,and food is the medium of exchange. Chas. Forde's bile beans keep the balance on the right side. Where they are used, the store of energy supplied by the food is properly maintained or banked up. Where they are not used, sometimes there's an overdraft on the system's energy, and the sufferer has to give way and fail. This failure is as serious as any business bankrupWy. Sometimes far more so. Take the case of Miss Polly Tweddle, of Portland-road. Newcastle-on-Tyne. Her digestive organs became dis- ordered to suet. an extent that she could not retain food, her strength failed, and she broke down com- pletely. Thanks, however, to bile beans, her health is now perfect, and she told the following facts to a local reporter :—" I suffered acutely from indigestion for over twelve months, and although at first it did not lessen my appe- tite I had a distressing sickness after each meal. Nothing I could eat would stay upon my stomach. Then my appetite fell away, and I grew thin and became so weak I could not walk many yards without assistance. I was under a doctor for some months, but as I made no improvement I looked else- where for relief, though I had really begun to believe I would never recover my health. I was advised to try bile beans, and I did so. They made a difference in me in a few weeks, and gradually my indigestion and weakness disappeared. Thanks to bile beans, I fully recovered my health and strength, and am now as well as anyone could wish to be.

It is useless te think that one can continue to expend energy by walking and working,and the hundred and one actions of everyday life, if energy is not being stored up in the human bank ; and if food is not digested properly, sooner or later there is bound to be a breakdown. Bile beans will prevent this, as in the case of Miss Tweddle, reported above. They are obtainable from all medicine vendors at one-and three-halfpence or two-and-nine per box; but you are warned against useless, if not actually harmful, substitutes and imitations. Substitutes never cure, and when purchasing it is advisable to bear this in mind.

A ROMANCE OF THE BOER WAR.

_ It is stated that during the war two Boers became detached from a commando in a northern desert beyond Lydenburg, and having wandered about and nearly perished from thirst came across the skeleton of a man with two heavy bags beside it, one containing gold and the other diamonds, which treasures the Boers buried, subsequently finding their commando. After peace, these two Boers went to secure the buried treasure. One returned, but the other, Van Nankerk, has been missing for three months, and foul play is feared, as an expedi- tion which set out to look out for the treasure and seek traces of the missing man, found neither, but during the journey learned much to engender strong suspicion. The Criminal Investigation Department is said to have the matter in hand.

A terrible snowstorm occurred in the Canton of Schwiz. Switzerland, on Sunday evening. Hundreds of trees were uprooted and thousands of windows were broken. The fruit crop is utterly ruined. For more than an hour after the storm hailstones as large as hazel nuts were lying six inches thick on the ground. Seventy-seven more passive resisters were summoned before the Sheffield Stipendiary on Wednesday, when the defendants included a magistrate and several leading Nonconformist ministers. In all the eases orders were made for distress warrants.

CHAPTER XVI:

WHY TREDEGAR DID NOT LEAVE ENGLAND.

WHEN his visitors had departed Tredegar crept back to his miserable bed of straw and clenched his hands with impotent rage. Their visit had been an insult to his help- lessness. If one hand had been free, nay, ii even his feet had been free, he would have flung himself upon them, and beater] them to death. Yet there was one that he knew had not come to mock him. He had caught a glimpse of bright hair in the background. Cynthia Cantrip had not come there to gloat over his misery, and as he thought of her, a thrill of hope ran through his weary body. Her infinite resource and unfailing energy might once again be used on his behalf. The debt he owed her, already too large to pay, might be still further increased. He had no doubt that she was planning his escape. He had felt the soft touch of her fingers at his feet in the boat, and realised that even then she had not, overlooked the smallest possibility of freeing him from his bonds. And how he had repaid her. Even now, in his lonely prison, he felt ashamed that he should think of .her at all. Every thought should be given to the woman he loved, and who, he felt sure, would do as much and more for him, if it were in her power. "Poor Mavanwy," he said to himself, she is but a child, and has neither the brain to conceive, nor the strength to execute. Yet she has all my heart, and if Cynthia were to lay down her life for me, she could not have that which I have already given." Poor Tredegar ! His mind was racked between a sense of gratitude and an honest love which takes no account of favOurs re- ceived or to come. Then ke smiled at his own thoughts. What mattered love or gratitude to one in his posi- tion—to a man whose days were in all proba- bility numbered. The shadow of death blotted out everything else. All earthly hopes and fears and passions grew dim under the dark wings of Azrael. He would die like a dog thrown into a pond with a stone about its neck. From his early boyhood he had always dreamed of an heroic death—a 'last • good fight with men falling dead around him like leaves shaken from a tree. If only he had . been calm and self-possessed on that terrible night at the Plas Tredegar ! If he had only stood his ground and faced his trial he might have been a free man to-day! Now he could hardly hope for a verdict of "Not guilty." Only Cynthia Cantrip's evidence would be in his favour and the -lying tongue of her father , would out-weigh that. It would be sus- pected that she was prejudiced and that she lied for love of him.

For two hours he lay in the darkness., turn. ing over these and other thoughts in his mind. The oppressive silence was only broken by the faint sound of a rat nibbling at one of the old wine bins. At last even that ceased, and Tredegar felt as though he had lost a com- panion in his loneliness. His mind idly turned to the days of his boyhood; when •he used to bring his fox terrier down into the Cellar and hunt the rats out of a pile of old boxes in the corner. He would have given all his worldly goods to feel the cold nose of that terrier now against his hand. The dark: ness and the silence were terrible. He began to whistle, but the notes sounded harsh and discordant, and the tune died away on his lips. Then he tried to sleep, for he was worn out, but his brain was too active, and his wounds were painful, and the irons on his wrists and ankles had begun to cramp his muscles. It was impossible to lie down in any comfort. Then it suddenly struck him that one of the policemen might be outside, and if so, that it would be good to hear his voice even if it were only raised in anger or rebuke. He called out, but there was no response. Then he took a draught of water and lay quite still. At last he heard a faint movement outside the door, and what seemed like the whisper of voices. He started up, and saw a thin line of light along the floor. Then the listened, every nerve strained, to catch ' the sound. For half a minute there was silence, then he heard the click of the lock, and a stfeak of yellow came through the half opened door. "Who is that ? " he said in a whisper. "Hush," was the low reply. Then a woman's fingers came into view. At first Tredegar thought it was Cynthia, and when he saw that it was Maxamwy his heart was filled with a great joy. She quietly closed the door behind her and advanced with the candle in her hand.

"My darling I" murmured Emrys, rising to his knees and stretching out his ~m.anacled hands towards her; She looked at him in terror. He was not a pleasant sight for anyone to gaze upon, and least of all for the girl- who loved him. Then her tender heart was filled with a great pity, and she longed to take Min in her arms. But, remembering her oath, she kept a little distance from him, and placed the candle on the floor..

" Emrys," she said, in a trembling voice, "I have come to release, you! There is no time to lose 1"

She then, took the key from her pocket, and, unlocking the handcuffs, laid them quietly on the straw. Then she knelt down and unfastened the manacles ..on his ankles. He rose to his feet, and tried to clasp her in his arms. But she evaded him, and held out her hands as though to ward off a blow. . A look of pain and surprise crossed his Lace.

" Mavanwy," he said, "my darling, why There is no • time to lose," she. exclaimed, hurriedly. " You must be miles away from here. by daybreak. No, Emrys please— remember how late it is I You must fly at once!"

"My own brave tittle, girl," be said, ten- derly. " I shall owe you my life." "You owe me nothing, Emrys," she replied, nicking up the candle and moving towards he door. • - .

He stood still, and then she turned round and gave him such a look of agony that a pain seemed to shoot through his heart. "What is it, Mavanwy ?" he said, "My darling, what is it ? Why do you look at me like that ?" And he came close to her side, and once more tried to take her in his arms. She shrank from him, and cowered against the wall, covering her face with one hand and weeping bitterly.: " Mavanwy t Mavanwy !" he said, in despair. He began to think that her troubles had affected her brain. She raised her head, aknd looked at him with a tear-stained facei

Emrys.'?. she faltered, "to-night you must leave the neighbourhood J As soon as possible you must leave Englanda We eau never be married, and you must forget me,3

"I do not understand I" he said, hoarsely. "Why can we never be married? My inno- cence will be proVed some day." "Your guilt or innocence will not matter, Emrys," she replied. "We . can never be married—at least, not for many years. I cannot tell you more. Now you must go !" "I will not go," he said, doggedly, unless you explain matters." `e For shame, ,Emrys," she replied, "You are not such a coward as that. I have risked everything—have given up everything to release you— Have you no gratitude ? Are you Pl

no, Mavanwy 1" he broke in. "I will go."

She paissed quickly through the door, and he followed her. She locked the door behind her, and they made their way up the steps and crept silently to the dining-room. Tredegar opened one of the windows. It was a less noisy Iwneans of exit than through the hall-door, .which was bolted and barred and chained. , Then he hesitated with his hand upon the window-sill. Mavanwy blew out the light and they were in darkness. I-Es great figure was silhouetted against the sky. " Mavanwy," helk said, earnestly, "I owe you my life! I will keep it for you. Ido not ask you to tell me what has come between us. I am sure that you have acted for the best. "

"I have acted for the best," she said, in a low voice. " I am sure it is for the best, Emrys. You must leave England at once. Here is money," and she pulled out the bundle of notes. Tredegar looked at them doubt- fully. "They have been lent to me," she continued, "to give to you." "I cannot take them," Tredegar said, knowing who .had lent them. "You would not offer them to me if yoU knew everything." "I know everything," she said; and you must take these for my sake."

Tredegar drew in his tireath sharply. He seemed to see everything more clearly. Mavanwy had learnt all about Cynthia, and had thought it best to break off their engage- ment."

never loved her, Mavanwy !" he said, hoarsely. "I have always been true to your" "I know—l. know," she said. " But you must go at once, Emrys, or someone will hear us. If you do not take these notes you will cause me lifelong misery. for, you cannot get out of England without them." He took them without a word and placed them in his pocket. "You know that I love you, and always shall love you," he said.

"I .know," she answered. "Good-bye, Emrvs," and she held out her hand.

He seized it in his powerful grasp, and drawing her head close to him, bent down his head to kiss her. She turned her face away, and sobbed as though her heart would break. She longed to fling her arms round his neck, and her whole soul yearned for one last embrace, one long passionate kiss—one fond good-bye. For a moment she hesi- tated, and the burden of her oath seemed light as a feather, to be blown away in the wind of passion. Then she shuddered, and shrank away from .him with a low cry. He let go of her hand, and climbed out of the window: "Good-bye, dearest," he said. "Good-bye, Emrys !" she replied; " and God bless you !" He wrung her hand in silence, and then ran swiftly across the lawn into the shadow of some trees. She watched him till he had disappeared, and for more than a quarter of an hour she stood at the open window and stared at the place where he had vanished 4. Then she closed the window and crept quietly up to bed. In half an hour she had cried herself to sleep.

For five days Tredegar made his way south, travelling by night, and sleeping in some lonely place by day. He dared not take the+ train, for he knew that every station 'would be watched. He was doubtful if it would be possible to get away from any port unob- served. His great stature marked him out everywhere amongst his fellow-men. His only hope seemed to lie in shipping as a stowaway to some French port, and thence booking a passage to South America. At any rate, he was furnished with the sinews of war, 'and could fight a hard and long battle 'against his _enemies with some chance of success.

But his mind was racked by a hundred doubts and fears that had no connection with his own safety. Hour after hour he tried to imagine how Mavanwy had obtained the keys to release him, and puzzled his •brain for some explanation of her conduct. It had seemed as though all her love had sud- denly died out. She had not given him a single loving word. She had shrunk from his embraces. and had hardly dared to look him in the face. And out of the chaos of thoughts two terrible suspicions rose upper- most in his mind. One, that Cynthia had told Mavanwy the whole story of those days in River Street; and tie other, that John Walroyd loved Mavanwy, and that she had obtained the keys from him by promising to be his wife.

He reached Cardiff in safety, but knew that the most perilous portion of his journey had nOw commenced. He purchased a sailor's suit in a low quarter of the town, shaved off his beard and moustache, and took a room in one of the lodging-houses frequented by the roughest sailors. Everyone stared at him, and he could not understand why he had not been recognised by the police, if any description of him had been forwarded to them. He did not know that the day after his departure, the monster had made an appearance in the Llynglas woods, and that he, Tredegar, was still supposed to be in the neighbourhood of Garth. The next evening, however, he found an old copy of the South Wales Echo in the bar of a public house, and the first headline that struck his eye_ was the following :

" FURTHER DEVELOPMENT OF THE CARDIGAN MYSTERY. MURDER OF A JUSTICE OF THE PEACE. •

DISAPPEARANCE OF HIS DAUGHTER."

Then, as he read the matter underneath, his face went white as death. and he staggered- against the wall bf a house, clutching the paper, and trying to read it with trembling hands. His brain reeled as he read the horrible news, and the lines swam into a misty blackness. For Mavanwy Morgan had disappeared, and Mr. Morgan had been found dead. And the paper was four days old. That very night he concealed himself under the tarpaulin of a truck, and, went rattling northwards again through the darkness.

And then at last, her strong will broke down and flinging herself on the bed, she cried like a little child, and prayed that God might strike her dead for all the evil she had done.

MR. WHITAKER WRIGHT.

Of more than middle height, inclined to corpulence, and of somewhat robust build, such is the appearance of Mr. Whitaker Wright to-days He has changed consider- ably since he last appeared before the British public. During his stay abroad he has grown a beard of brown tinged with iron grey, Which considerably modifies the faeial aspect, but by no means tends to conceal identity. A somewhat receding forehead is backed by a head slightly bald in front, but bearing in the °tato pacts ismown hair tinged, as the beard is, with coming grey. A rather sallow com- plexion, full cheeks, average nose, prominent lips, and strong neck complete the descrip- tion of this man of the moment. Mr. Whit- aker Wright is oae of the most unassuming millionaires we have. He dress'es modestly in black, abhors jewellery, and always gives the appearance of desiring to be as little seen as a man of eighteen-stone can reasonably expect. But Mr. Wright enjoys the beauti- ful, and is very house-proud. His most cher- ished residence is Lea Park, near Goclalming. Lea Park almost touches the unattainable, even fov a millionaire. It is an Aladdin's palace—a palace that is estimated to have cost something like £250,000. When Mr. Wright acquired the estate some seven years ago, he gave his architects and contractors orders to employ 500 workmen at one tiine, and entered upon a scheme of building wad beautification which would have required twelve years to complete. But his ambition to make his residence perfect in his own eyes has, of course, been held in abeyance for a time, and the workmen have ceased their revivifying. There are a great number of unopened cases of valuable Roman statuary lying in a field close by which are not im- proving by their exposure. But Mr. Wright Cannot hefp this.

AUSTRALIA'S NEW GOVERNOR-GENERAL The Right Hon. Lord Northcote, G.C.1.E., C. 8., Governor of Bombay, who has been accepted as Governor-General of the Common- wealth of Australia in December next, to suc- ceed Lord Tennyson on his resignation of the office, is the second son of the first Earl of Iddesleigh, better known as Sir Stafford Northcote, and was born in 1846, and mar- ried in 1873 the adopted daugh- ter of Baron Mount Stephen. He was attached to the Marquess of Ripon's Spe- cial Mission to arrange the Ala- bama Treaty. and was Secre- tary to the Claims Commis- sion under the Treaty of Wash- ington until RT. HON. LORD NORTHCOTE. 1873. He ac- companied Lord Salisbury on his Embassy to Constantinople in 1876. and was then appointed acting Third Secretary in the Diplomatic Service. He was his father's private secretary when Chancellor of the Exchequer in 1877-o; Financial Secretary to the War Office, 1885-86; Surveyor-General of Ordnance, 1886-87; and Charity Commissioner, 1891-92. He was created C.B. in 1880 and baronet in 1887. He sat in the House of Commons as Conservative member for Exeter from 1880 until October, 1899, was appointed Governor of Bombay and made a baron of the United Kingdom in 1900.

THE NEW NAVAL COLLEGE.

Our illustration of the new Naval college at Osborne, just opened by the King, will give a complete idea of the extensive buildings which have thus been placed at the disposal of the navy, thanks to the gracious interest of His Majesty, who handed over several acres of the Royal estate at Osborne for the purpose. The new college is near the road between Fast Cowes and Whippingham, and it is expected that the cadets will go into residence at the college next September. The buildings

THE NEW NAVAL COLLEGE AT OSBORNE. Which were the Royal stables have been metamorphosed into a cadets' dining-room nearly molt. long, with class and lecture rooms, laboratories, and professors' rooms. There is a gymnasium 17oft.' long, completely fitted up with all- the latest and best appliances, and a very large recreation room. The officers quarters are handsome and con- venient. In the anteroom is a representation of the Victory, with the motto beneath which 'Nelson's signal at Trafalgar. Captain E. Wemvss is in command at the college.

THE PONTIPP'S VITAL ORGANS.

One of the strangest of the many ceremonies connected with the interment of a pope is the Preservation of the deceased pontifi's viral organs after the body has been duly embalmed. This was duly done in the case of the late ?ope Leo; and the vital organs were placed in a large crystal vase and sealed in the presence °I an apostolic notary. Then they were con- veyed to a crypt beneath the Vatican Basilica ,nd interred beside those of Pius IX. Previous to 1870 these urns were deposited in the vaults cd the Church of Saints Vincent and Anastasius, being conveyed thither through the public streets with great ceremony but now, of _co. urse, that has.all been changed by political "rontristances. The new Pope, in the mean- tin) e, continues to create a highly. favourable Ampression, and delights all with whom he

THE LONDON VICTORIA MEMORIAL.

An interesting piece of work has been commenced in London during the summer vacation: this is the splendid memorial to Queen Victoria, which is to stand at the end of the Mall, facing Buckingham Palace;. but it will be many years before it is finally com- pleted, as it will be a very big affair when finished. What is being done first is the levelling of a semi-circular plot of .sikty feet in diameter, and the widening of the end of the Mall. Between twenty and thirty trees have to be removed, and this first step is not trcpected to be completed until November. The centre of this large plot will be occupied Mr. Brooks' enormous statue of the late Queen, which will form the central piece and

FINISHED.,

pivot of the whole '.design., The rest of the scheme will be from-the plans of Mr. Aston Webb, the latest R.A. and well-known archi- tect. It will consist of some five and twenty marble .figures and groups surrounding the principal statue, while the whole semi-circle, which will also contain fountains, footpaths, carriage roads and grass plots, will be enclosed by a balustrade. The inner marble wall will contain flights of steps at the back and front leading up to the monument, and the balus- trade itself will be decorated with .groups of statuary representing • the British colonies. The long delay will be due to the immense amount of sculptor's work involved ; but when complete, the. result will be very fine and imposing—worthy, indeed, of the great and prosperous reign it will mark.

TEE OTER IN TEE MIL

Whatever is the ultimate fate of the motor car. in private life, it is gradually becoming recognised that its utilitarian 'value for tran- sit is very great. For van work and long- distance ro urneys each :month sees it in greater favour; and it looks as though it would be widely used ere long for military purposes. In addition to the. War Office cars And the new Motor Volunteer . Corps, a new automobile .is on trial for Army work, and it promises very Well, as it will not only be handy, but compact and complete. The car, of which we give an illustration, is built to carry a thousand rounds of ammunition, to- gether with four riflemen and four rifles, in addition to their rations for four days and sufficient petrol for a two hundred mile run.

It will also carry two waterproof tents, blan- kets, entrenching - shovels, • and other minor military appliances. It will, in fact, if adopted, be a miniature motor blockhouse! The question of the automobile as a vehicle of pleasure is again the topic of the moment, and Mr. Walter Long's new Motor Bill had a very rough passage through the House of Com- mons. It had to be very considerably altered before it was acceptable to the majority; and great surprise was expressed by the enormous amount of prejudice excited by the subject. Thoughtless and inconsiderate drivers have served to render the motor unpopular to an unfair degree; but it must, on the other hand, be remembered that each year sees it less of a rich man's toy and more- of a public neces- sity, which will owe its development to the purse of the rich, who are ready to pay the fancy prices in the days of infancy of a great Industry.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 15, 1903.

THE WATER COMMITTEE AND ITS BUSINESS. :BRE Amur THE SLACK C9NTRACT.

Mr, C. W. Davenports Account.

interesting Discussioa.

More information regarding the now notorious "slack contract " was forthcoming at the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening, and the ordinary business of the Water Committee also gave rise to considerable discussion. The committee recom- mended that Alderman Smethurst be appointed a mem- ber in the place of the late Councillor Baird.—At a meeting of the Water Committee,held on the 6th July, a letter was read from Mr. George Mitchell, resigning his position as assistant engineer in consequence of his having obtained a position with the contractor for the Tynemouth storage reservoir. It was resolved that the resignation be accepted,and the request of Mr.Mitehell that he should be relieved from his duties as soon as possible be complied with, so soon as the Chairman is satisfied that adequate arrangements have been made for the work of the department being efficiently carried on; also that at the request of the Fire Engine Com- mittee instructions be given to the reservoir keeper at Appleton to increase the supply of water upon receiving information from the superintendent of a fire within the borough.—Ata meeting held on August sth a report by the water engineer on the supply of slack to the Winwick and Delph-lane pumping stations a summary of which appeared in last Saturday's" Examiner," was re- ceived and ordered to be printed. The following questions were addressed to the Chair- man of the Committee (Alderman Francomb) by Coun- cillor Lord :—At the last meeting of the Town Council you informed the members that from June 16th to the 7th of July the Slate Company had only supplied nine tons 12cwt. of slack. (1). Is it not a fact that the quantity actually supplied amounted to 65 tons Scwt ? (2). Who supplied you with the figures presented to the Council at its last meeting, and what explanation have you to offer thereon? Alderman FRANCOMB, in replying to the first question said he informed the Council that the Company supplied a quarter of a cwt more than was stated in Councillor Lord's question. In regard to the quantity actually supplied he accepted Councillor Lord's figures, viz,. 65 tonsiScwt. As to the second question—who supplied those figures ?—the figures were supplied to him by Mr. Mitehell, who had been left in charge by Mr. Dees. The Town Clerk had written to Mr. Mitchell for an explana- tion, and, perhaps, they would allow him to read Mr. Mitchell's reply received from the post he had recently gone to. That reply was as follows : • (ooP v.) "Font Reservoir, near Ewesley, Northumberland, 27-7-1903. Dear sir,—l am in receipt of yours of the 25th inst., and am a little surprised at your applying to me for information seeing that I was m?rely an onlooker at an interview between Mr. Deas and the Chairman when the statement you refer to was made. As I have not full particulars of the question by me I can only say in reply that. the approximate figure of ten tons referred to the quantity of slack delivered by Messrs. the Warrington Slate Co., at Delph-lane P.S., between June 22nd and July 2nd, the former date being the date of the committee meeting when the question first came before the Water Com- mittee after being raised at the June meeting of the Council. Previous to June 22nd, we had, of course, no instructions from the committee in the matter, but on that date Messrs. R. Evans and Co. were, at the Chairman's instigation, instructed to commence deliver- ing slack at Winwick P.S. On June 24th, Mr. Deas, wrote to Messrs. The Warrington Slate Co., asking them to stop delivering at Winwick P.S., but to continue delivering at Delph-lane P.S. Messrs. The Warrington Slate Co. stopped delivery at Delph-lane on Thursday, 2nd July, and before any more was delivered after the Walking Day holidays, I at the Chairman's instigation, instructed Messrs. The Warrington Slate Co., not to deliver any more slack at Delph-lane P.S. I trust the above explanation will make matters clear. 112 any case, I can assure you that the information supplied by me to the Chairman was given in absolute good faith. I shall be pleased to supply any further information you may desire and am, Yours (signed), GEORGE MITCHELL, J. Lyon Whittle Esquire, Town Clerk, Warrington. Continuing, the ALDERMAN said they had all had Mr Deas' report and he hoped they had all been able to understand it. Mr. Mitchell had certainly mixed up things very much differently from what he (the speaker) understood them to be. On the 16th June he (Alder- man Francomb) answered Councillor Lord's qu9stions to the best of his ability and without the slightest attempt to equivocate. Next morning he went to Mr. Deas' office and told the water engineer that he must at once give the orders to the original oon- tractors.—Messrs. Richard Evans and Company. If there was anything wrong he must report and they would call a special meeting and settle the matter. A week afterwards—on the 23rd— , he WWI at Winwick and saw Mr. Mitchell. He said to Mr. Mitchell, "How is the slack suiting?" and without ' any hesitation Mr. Mitchell replied "Oh, very well indeed, Mr. Chairman." He (the alderman) said 'ls it keeping plenty of steam?" and the reply was "Oh yes. The only complaint I have to make is that it is a little bit fine at times." A week afterwards he saw Councillor Lord's questions and he was at a loss to know whatever they were to put on the agenda for. He at once went to the waterworks and saw Mr. Mitchell again. He (Alderman Francomb) said "Surely you have not got slack from anyone else but Richard Evans and Company?" Mr. Mitchell replied- "Oh, yes, we have. The Slate °company sent to Mr. Deas and said they had a few tons of washed slack and had not customers for it, and he has agreed to take it." He (Alderman Francomb) said "I am exceedingly sorry you have done anything of the sort Without consulting the committee." At the last Council meeting be understood that only a small amount of slack had been supplied by the Slate Company and he thought that under the circumstances there was nothing very wrong about it. He fancied the Council agreed with him. At the interview he asked Mr. Mitchell what quantity the Slate Comp_any had supplied and he said "Oh, a few tons." He asked Mr. Mitchell to let him know what the 'amount was in order that he could give the , information to the Council. Halwas informed that the quantity was 9 tons 12 cwts 1 quarter audit was supplied from June 17th to a day or two before the last meeting of the Council. He naturally thought that was an end of it. At a subsequent meeting of the Water Committee however, when the bills came in he saw that there was a bill from the Slate iCompany for £7l. This at once alarmed him and he wondered whatever it was for. He reckoned the figures up and found that instead of the quantity being 9 tons 12 cwts 1 qr, it was considerably over 50 tons. He at once acquainted the Committee with that information. How the officials mixed up Delph Lane, with Winwick and all those things he could not understand. He distinctly said they must get the whole of their requirements for the Water Committee from Richard Evans and Com- pany, and if the slack did not suit bring the mattes before the Committee and let them know all about it. It was very annoying to him and the Committee. Ho told the Council at the last meeting that his first infor- mation about Messrs. Evans and Company not supply- ing the Water Committee's requirements was when he read Councillor Lord's questions. It was utterly impos- ' sible for either the Chairman or the members of the Committee to know what they were receiving after they had bought goods through the Council When they bought slack, setts, or anything else it was the business of the head official to see that the com- mittee got what they bought, and if there was any- thing wrong to bring it forward. Unless that was done it was antutter impossibility to carry on the work of the Council. He would be very glad to answer any ques- tions and he was exceedingly sorry that "this thing had been bundled and muddled up as it had been.' In , moving the adoption of the minutes Alderman Fran- comb explained that the reason why Alderman Smethurst was proposed as a new member was becausehe was chairman of the Fire Engine Committee ,If anyone ought to be on the Water Committee it was the chairman of the Fire Engine Committee, so that if Ithere was an outbreak of fire he would know where the hydrants were to be found. (Laughter.) That was not an idle statement. When 'Skelton's fire took place there was a terrible lot of trouble to find the hydrants., He almost thought the two committees should be amalganiated as it was necessary that the Fire Engine Committee should know where the hydrants were.

Councillor F. R. ROBERTS asked why, if Alderman Francomb was so exceedinglyanxious that the muddling should be stopped, he did not send word to the Slate Company of the desire of the committee that this thing should not go any further. It seemed to him that if Alderman Francomb had taken the trouble to write a letter to the . Company or in some other way acquaint them with the feeling of the Council and committee the whole thing would have been stopped. People who had done nothing more than comply with orders they had received had been loaded with an amount of odium which he did not think they deserved. To be fair with the company whose name had been dragged through the mire during the con- troversy, if they had been told of tile wish of the Committee he Was perfectly satisfied they would have stopped supplying the slack. They had no wish to dis- pose of goods to the Council except in a fair and busi- nesslike way. lie contended that all members of the committee and the Council were just as anxious for the business to be conducted with propriety as were the Non-Traders and he protested against little slips being made the subject of election cries. Councillor LORD said he understood there had been a disputed account with the contractor (Mr. C. W. Daven- port) in regard to the Delph-lane waterworks. Were not the Council asked to pass an account which the Committee had passed that day against a Standing Order? The "MAYOR

The TOWN I.ERK said the form of contract was drawn up by the Master Builders' Association and the Associa- tion of Architects of England. It was adopted through- out the country and members of the Builders' Associa- tion could not sign any other. He was prepared to advise the Corporation to use it in all cases. The con- tract required the retention of 10 per cent. but this was reduced to 5 per cent. The contract also provided that the contractor should be entitled to receive payment of all moneys within three months of the completion of the work or from the date of the Corporation taking possession. The Corporation had had possession since August of last year. Further discussion ensued, Alderman Smethurst re- marking that he supposed Councillor Pemberton was fond of receiving writs. The minutes were then confirmed. THE NEW MASTER'S CLERK. Mr. Healey having resigned the position of Master's Clerk, it was resolved that Mr. Alfred Turpin, aged 22, who was recommended by the Master, be offered the appointment at a salary of £3O per annum (being a , reduction of £lO from present salary) with the usual emoluments. AN APPOINTMENT AND A RESIGNATION. Miss BEATRICE DURNING, was appointed laundress at an annual salary of £3O with the usual emoluments. A letter wag read from the officers' cook resigning her position. The resignation was accepted. THE • EXTENSION OF THE CASUAL WARDS. The CLERK produced the contract for the extension of casual wards, signed by Mr. Joseph Dolan. The seal of the Board wasorderedto be affixed thereto. The amount of the contract is £550. THE NEW COUNTY RATE. The CLERK stated that he had not yet received the County rate call but he gathered from a report of the meeting last week that exclusive of the question of the Education Act and its onerations, the ordinary expendi- ture of the county had increased. This was not a very gratifying feature, especially when they considered that the ratable arop, was decreasing whilst the expenditure was increasing. He noticed that it was decided at the meeting to grant the chief officers £3OO each in respect of extra services. The Clerk was to receive £2,303 instead of £2,000; the county treasurer £1,300 instead of £l,OOO and the county auditor also got £3OO extra. The assessable value of the county had dropped from £9,110,856 in 1902 to £8,999,820 this year or a decrease of £111,036. The Chairman pointed out that notwith- standing the reduced assessable value there was a material increase in ordinary expenditure. That meant the old story that the county rate would be increased. Mr. J. W. KNOWLES : Everything points to it going up. , • The CLERK said it was necessary that the county rate notices should be signed that day, leaving himto fill in the amount when he heard what the rate was to be. This course was adopted. The Board then rose. WARRINGTON POLYGLOT SOCIETY.

01 Frid ty evening last the Warrington Polyglot Society met at Sto -*toe He Ith and proceeded by cycle to Arley Hill, tvdere arrangentente had been made for their viewing the gardens. Some fourteen members started at 8.45 and an unfortunate fifteenth followed about an hour later. Weather and roads Were ideal, and the fourteen arrived quickly at their destination. After a short delay in storing machines, they proceeded through th 3 grounds under the genial guidance of Mr. Smith. It VIM noticed that the members expressed their appreciation of the triumphs of horticultural skill that crowded ape!' the n, entirely in the English language, and it is understood that some prophetic instinct prompted them to reserve their powers in other tongues for later use. The grassy surfaces, to the eye and foot alike, smooth and perfect as a billiard table; the beautifully kept hedges; the rose garden and the delightful her'oaceaus borders are sufficient to exhaust the vocabulary of any one language, and it was with a feeling of powerlessness to express further admiration that the party was led through the older buildings which saw the first Btuart ascend the Nttglish throne, and so in the gathering gloom of twilight, with the rising moon glancing fitfully through fanta4tic do ud-shapes, they were delivered, happy and unsuspecting into the jaws of the Arley maze. The hedges here had been long un- clipped, and frequently the path seethed blocked with foliage, which however yielded to pressure and closed again behind the onward moving figures. The more ardent and enthusiastic spirits speedily forged ahead, and with their onward progress, confidence . grew; from a dignified walk they broke into a run, only to be broaght to a dead stop by an impenetrable barrier of greenery. Again and again they tried'; again and again they failed and it was here the variety of tongues found vent. The English language was exhausted. In vain rushing to and fro the members of the Society grew equally worn out, and French and German are apt media for expres- sive use of language under such circumstances. It was at this Stage the puzzled wanderers were collected by the amused master of ceremonies, f!andleach hanging on to the tail of his predecessors attire, the whole company was led at the trot round sudden centre-of-gravity- upsetting corners, and battered by springing branches the goal was reached, and a well-earned rest enjoyed. This was too soon broken by a supposed villainous attempt Of the guideto slip out unobserved, and again the wild rush commenced; again the fierce battering by branches pashed aside and suddealy released by the one before. The entrance was safely reached however, with slightcasualtiea, chiefly in the form of damaged hats, and in spite of the arduous labours of the maze it was decided to ride home via Arley Mill and High Legh. Only those who know the road to the mill can realize the mild excitement of a ride along a pretty road in semi-darkness, the lamp's uncertain light scarcely sufficing to show the little traps awaiting the unwary. Here a deeper rut engulfs a wheel and a Wild swoop. to escape a fall nearly lands one rider ma fieldof oats. Further on a couple of gates call foran undxpected dismount, and at the awkward descent and sharp turn at the mill pond, we posb a sentinel to safeguard rash riders against an impromptu bath. But it was worth the trouble and risk. Those who have only Seen the mill-pond in broad daylight know only half its beaeties. in the light of the now clear-sailing moon, the scene was a fairyland, and re- gretfully it was left behind as in a long and straggling line the homeward run through High Legh• was com- menced. Many were the enquiries made after the ex- pected fifteenth rider who never reached the company. His history is sad and can be briefly indicated. ht harm- less looking hairpin, carelessly dropped by some fair deity—perhaps one of our own party—penetrated his tyre soon after he left the, Appleton cross-road. OR in- vestigation he found he lacked a necessary part of his repair outfit, and his comments were all in the German language. At least, the Appleton natives say they never heard such language ! It is recommended that all intel- ligent cyclists consider the advisability of attaching themselves to the Polyglot Society. Should some en- quiring Member of any of our learnedsocieties, in pur- suit of folk-lore, hear some new legend of a ghostly wanderer who at midnight 'hour with flickering light and occasional metallic clank wends his way through lonely Stretton,muttering wildly unintelligible gutturals he will do wisely to apply to the Polyglot Society for fuller information before publishing his tale of mystery. It was long after midnight when the fifteenth member reached his longed-for couch that night.

They don't frighten me.

Alderman SMETHURST : I don't want to-have them. On the vote being taken the amendment was lost,only Councillors James Evans, Barton, Dodds, Horrocks, Lord, Pemberton and Thornton voting for it. In reply to Councillor F. R. Roberts, Alderman FRANCOMB said he did not blame the Slate Company in the least, neither did any member of the committee. The company simply did as other tradesmen would have done. They received an order and supplied the goods. He knew nothing whatever about the 65 tons until he saw the bill the other day. He naturally thought that tke figures Mr. Mitchell gtve him were correct, so he had no opportunity of communicating with the Slate Company. If he had known that they were supplying 65 tons he should have communicated with them. Con- tinuing the Alderman said he was informed that Mr. Davenport's contrast *as completed and the work was simply being measured up. Councillor PEMBERTON said he had no intention of blaming the Slate Company ot Alderman Francomb. He thought the blame rested with the management of the water department. The minutes were then confirmed. The consideration of a motion by Councillor Pember- ton "that a committee be appointed to investigate the last three Slack Contracts for Winwick and Delph-lane waterworks," was adjourned until the next meeting.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL

Sockto:i Heath's Representation.

MOPE Parish Councillors.

A meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held in the Board Room at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday morning. Mr. S. Woodward, J.P., presided,and there were also present :—Messrs. G. Pilling (vice- chairman), J. Mclntyre, T. Darlington, P. Gleave, Admiral Jones-Parry, T. Baxter, C. E. Linaker, T. Wright, J. B. Chadwick, J. Moseley Williams, E. Clarke, J. Edwards, J. Spruce, A. Greenway; with G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. Rough (assistant clerk), Dr. Adams (medical officer of health), W. Farrington (inspector), andJ. Swinton (surveyor).

STOCKTON HEATH'S REPRESENTATION.

The Clerk read the minutes Of the last meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee when it was re- solved :—"That the District Council be asked to kindly reconsider their decision respecting an additional dis- trict councillor for Stockton Heath, and that a sub- committee be formed by the District Council, together with Mr. C. NV. Davenport, Mr. J. Modnfield and the overseers for Stockton Heath, to investigate fully the necessity for an additional representative on the District Council, and report to a, future meeting of the District Council."

The Chairman said a question of standing orders was affected by the reconsideration of the representation of Stockton Heath. Within a period of six months the Council had come to a decision on the matter and before they could adopt the proposal contained in the minutes another motion would be necessary.

On the motion of Mr. Pilling, -sec—onjciecl by Mr. Mclntyre, it was decided to re-open the question. Mr. Pilling moved that the old Committee—Messrs.. Woodward, Knowles, Stokes, Darlington, and Pilling— together with the gentlemen recommended by the Parochial Committee, be appointed to reconsider the question. He remarked that he thought the P arochial Committee meant either to have a Council for Stockton Heath or an additional representative. The resolution was agre3d to. NEW STREETS AT STOCKTON HEATH. On the motion of Mr. Pilling, the Council decided to level, pave, metal and make good Dundonald Avenue and so much of Kimberley drive as extends from Walton road to Dundonald Avenue, Stockton Heath, under the Private Street Works Act. It was further resolved that the specifications, plans, sections &c, prepared and sub- mitted by the surveyor be approved, that the expenses be payable by equal annual instalments extending over a period of ten years with interest at the rate of £4 per cent per annum, and that application be made to the Local Governmeat Board for sanction to a loan for the required amount.

HONORARIUM FOR THE MEDICAL OFFICER. Mr. Moseley Williams reported that at a committee meeting held that morning it was unanimously agreed that an horora,rium of 100 guineas be granted to the Medical Officer for extra services rendered during the recent small-pox epidemic which lasted twenty-three weeks. He moved that this course be adopted. In reply to Mr. Linaker the Chairman said there were eleven cases of smallpox la the hospital during the epidemic and Dr. Adams had to visit the patients. lie pointed out that the ordinary duties of Medical Officer were simply connected with the carrying out of the Public Health Act and had nothing whatever to do with medical attendance on patients. During the time he visited the hospital, Dr. Adams had to employ an assis- tant to attend to his private practice. After some discussion the resolution was agreed to.

THE THELWALL NUISANCE.

Admiral Jones-Parry and tin Medical

Officer.

VOTE OF CENSURE PROPOSED BUT NT

SECON BED.

The much-discussed Thelwall • nuisance once more occupied the attention ot the membets of the Runcorn Rural District Council at the monthly meeting of that authority held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, under the presidency of Mr. Woodward. At the request of Admiral Jones-Parry, the Clerk read the minutes of the Council containing instructions upon which the Medical (nicer of Health (Dr. Adams) acted when he engaged Professor Delepine to make investigations as to the causes of the nuisance.

By the resolution in question, it appeared, Dr. Adams was instructed "to confer with the County Medical Officer of Health with a view to further investigating the alleged nuisance at Thelwall," and it was further resolved" that he be authorised to obtain such assist- anceand incur such expenses as he may deem necessary." Admiral Jones-Parry contended that the intention of the Council when they passed the resolution was that the assistance should be given by the of-1i cials and mem- bers of the Council, and more especially by himself as the Councillor for the immediate neighbourhood. That Dr. Adams should order Professor Delepine to come down and make a report on the matter was quite beyond the intention of the Council. In his opinion the reso- lution could not possibly justify such steps being taken. At every Council meeting they were informed that they were likely to have a report from the County Council, and Dr. Adams, in sitting quietly in the room and not, giving any information at all publicly that he had in- structed Professor Delepiue to report on the matter,was going far beyond his duty and acting with very great discourtesy to the Council. Had he known that Dr. Adams was going to engage Professor Delepine he should have raised his voice against it. The Chaiiman : Are you going to move a vote of censure on Dr. Adams?

Admiral Jones-Parry proceeded to contradict Professor Delepine's opinion that the nuisance was not a danger to health and concluded by moving a vote of censure on Dr. Adams for having withheld from the Council the instruction he gave to Professor De'opine. The resolution was not seconded and consequently fell to the ground, the Chairmin remarking that Dr. Adams stood before the Council perfectly free from any charge of injustice. Mr. Linaker said Admiral Jones-Parry was perfectly right in stating that no one understood that Dr. Adams would go so far as he did. ___ , .

The 'Clerk said that in zegard to the question of Dr. Adams having taken upon himself to engage Professor Delepine, if there was any blame at all he 'was afraid he (the clerk) would have to take it. When the resolution was passed he sent a copy to Dr. Adams with telephoned to him to ask whether the resolution went so far as to authorise him to engage any assistance he should deem necessary for the purpose of carrying out the resolution. He (Mr. Ashton) told the Medical Olicer that he con- sidered the resolution fully authorised him to engage any parson he considered necessary to make investiga- tion as he thought fit. If there Wil, 3 a question of any person exceeding his duty he was afraid he (Mr. Ashton) would have to take the blame. Mr. Pilling: I should move a vote of censure on the Clerk. (Laughter.) Mr. Linaker said he was not prepared to go so far as to support a vote of censure. He could quite see that there-might have been a misunderstanding. If Admiral Jones-Parry would put the resolution in a milder form he would second it.

Dr. Adams explained at he did not send written it; structions to Professor Delepine.When the Council passed the resolution they had received Dr. Penny's report. Professor Delepine had the whole of the materials be- fore him and his instructions were to report on the right or wrong of the allegations that the tannery was the source of the disease, and on the prevalence, of diphtheria._

i Admiral Jones-Parry said that he as therepresentative of the district might have been informed of the matter, and he would have offered himself as a witness and brought forward plenty of evidence that the Professor did not'seem to have:had trefore him.The!whole thing was done in a very underhand way. The matter then dropped. At a later stage of the proceedings it was reported that the Finance Committee had authorised the ray- merit of Professor Delepine's account of 105 guiueas and an account of £10: for printing the report.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 15, 1903.

even against iron from Germany, would ultimately produce that result. But if the direct proposal to tax food imports were abandoned what would be the position of a Conservative member who has defined his views with no more precision than- this? Would he consider himself at liberty to sup- port a proposal for the taxation of manufactured goods.? Nobody knows and nobody seems to take the trouble to inquire, for the confusion which reigns on the Unionist Benches in the House of Commons is repeated by the Unionist voters in the constituencies. What is to become of a Free Trade Unionist member or candidate in view of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S policy, whatever that may turn out to be? Is he toyield his seat without a struggle to some Protectionist of his own party; or may we expect to see Unionist electors who take different views of our fiscal policy voting for the same candidate, and thus making it impossible to determine what amount of support is being given to the Colonial Secretary ? So curious a situation as this has never before arisen in our political history, for we have never previ- ously had a Prime Minister who was avowedly without "settled convictions" on the dominant question of the hour. Even the Liberal party does not escape from some consequences of the state of doubt and uncertainty into which Mr. BALFOUR has plunged his own supporters. What, for in- stance, is the attitude to adopt towards members like Lord HUGH CECIL, or Sir EDGAR VINCENT, or Mr. IVOR GUEST, who are convinced Free Traders? To oppose them will be to help Mr. CHAMBERLAIN; but to support them will be to betray the cause of education. Should a general eleetimli take place before Parliament again meets all these questions will have to be settled at once in something resembling thq frame of mind in which the game of "family coach" is played. But the Liberal policy, we take it, is clear even in the case of the most pronounced of the Free Trade Conservatives. Liberals must necessarily look beyond the Free Trade issue to the legislation that must follow on a Chamberlain defeat. It will be impossible for them, therefore, to give support to or to refrain from opposing, any Free Trade candi- date that is not with them on the education ques- tion. The future must not be sacrificed to a temporary embarrassment. Where there is a situation such as that which exists at Exeter, where both candilates are Free Traders, and one in a most capable exponent of Liberal views on the education question, every Liberal vote must, of course, be given to the Liberal candidate.

MT. ANDREW CAR- MR. CARNEGIE AND NEGIE'S munificence is PROTECTION. tremendous. It is

3 astonishing the world. There has been nothing like it before. It is only in our own generation that such magnificent alms- giving has become pc.ssible. CRCE3US is reputed to have been very rich, and in the world of imagina- tion the gold and jewels of the Count of Monte Cristo has seemed to mock all possibility of rivalry in extent of lavishness of expenditure. But neither CRCE3US nor the Count of Monte Cristo had so much wealth at his command as the Pittsburg ironmaster. How did Mr. CARNEGIE make him- self the master of so many millions of money ? He was a poor Scotch bay, and now he could buy up nearly all Scotland. How has it come about in a business lifetime which has been far from unduly prolonged ? The answer to this question has a particularly close bearing on the subject of "the inquiry," concerning which Mr. BALFOUR was care- ful to disclose nothing in the House of Commons on Monday. Mr. CARNEGIE has been made a rich man partly by natural wit, of course, partly by the union of opportunity with natural sagacity, partly by enterprise but mainly by Protection.

"The history of the Carnegie Steel Company " is the latest book to awaken attention in the United States. It is written by Mr. JAMES HOWARD BRIDGE, who has been Mr. CARNEGIE'S private secretary, and has therefore seen a good deal of that side of money-making which is not disclosed to the public view. The New York correspondent of the "Standard" says of this book that" its importance lies not in its revelations of the seamy methods of accumulating wealth and organising the Steel Trust, but in the disclosure of trade secrets calculated to have powerful economic and political influence." For example, many private documents are given textually, in- cluding a letter from Mr. SCHWAB to Mr. FRICK, declaring that rails can be made in the United States for 12 dollars per ton and exported for 16 dollars. The tariff enables the Trust to sell its steel in the American market for 28 dollars. That is an astounding revelation. The Steel Trust is able to mike the steel user of the United States pay 28 dollars for what it produces for 12 dollars or less ! Why need we I wonder at the great wealth of Mr. CARNEGIE or the immense salary of Mr. SCHWAB, the largest ever paid since the buildings of the pyramids at least. The great w)rks at Pittsburg have been protected from competition, to ucli an extent that they have been able to charge to the citizen who protected them almost two and a half times as much as he ought to have paid. What have been the consequences? It would be simply im- possible to enumerate them in anything else than a volume. The farmer brEaking up the prairie in the Far West has had to pay from two to three times as much for his agricultural imple- ments as they would have cost him if America had been a Free Trade country. The extra cost has to be put on the price of the corn that he produces which is carried to the place of export over rails which have been bought at prices inflated by Protection, and is then shippedl off to the old world in vessels built in English 1 Ishipyards because the high prices of steel and iron ! will not permit the United States to compete in the shipbuilding industry. It is thus that under Protection the individual prospers at the expensel of the community, and that fortunes are amassed of such proportions as become a threat to the body politic. The just-mentioned fact about tho ship- buil ding trade may suggest matter for reflectio 'to those muddle-headed people who think that one ought to abandon Free Trade because it has not been adopted by other countries "Cobden was mistaken," they say. "He said that in a few years all the Norld would adopt Free Trade." Well COBDEN was too sanguine. He had a much higher opinion of the wisdom of mankind than subsequent events have justified. But it is far from being certain that the abandonment on tariff protection by other countries would be ad vantageous to ourselves. It is quite certain that our great shipbuilding industry would suffer severely if Americans did not tax themselves for the advantage of Mr. CARNEGIE and his partners. Protection makes it impossible for a protected
