THE EXAMINER.

AUGUST 15th 1903.

THE energy with

THE ABNORMAL which Parliament POLITICAL SITUATION, pushed along to- wards the holidays suggests much matter for reflection. There has been talk of Saturday sittings in both Houses. The Lords have kept steadily and resolutely at work till nearly midnight, and the Commons have gone home with the milk in the morning. So great-is the general eagerness to get away from legislative tail at the earliest possible moment and to begin the slaughter of " the sacred birds." Parlia- ment was prorogued yesterday and members of the House of Commons:have separated in consider- able uncertainty as to whether they will ever again be entitled to take their seats at Westminister. There are the most various anticipations of the political probabilities of the immediate future. M r BALFOUR. has so managed things that Parlia., ment must separate in the utmost confusion of mind alike as to the chances of an early general election, the nature of the intended appeal to the people and the leadership 'Hider which such an appeal would be made—the leadership, that is to say, of Mr. BALFOUR'S own side. Even Mr; CHAMBERLAIN'S views, definite as they were at the start, have become involved in prevailing cloud. The Colonial Secretary was, at the ontset,for pre- ferential tariffs in favour of the Colonies and the consequent taxation of food. This, he declared, is the only policy which can hold the Empire together. But the preferential tariffs and the taxation of food and the other items of rZollverein idea have tended to recede, and even to disappear like the old age pensions which they were to produce. It is doubtfu whether preference can any longer be described as Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S policy. The idea of pure Protectionism has usurped its place. Sir THOMAS WRIGHTSON, who wants to be protected against German iron, may be regarded as the type of man by 'whom the Colonial Secretary is being most zealously supported. Here and there a Conserva- tive member more independent than the rest,writes to his constituency to say that he will not be a party to any policy which will increase the cost o the food of the people. Any ',form [of Protection,

===== 0002601_19030815_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
country to compete with a Free Trade country where the conditions are equal except in respect to the tariff. That is one reason why we are able to pay for our food and our raw materials by our exports of manufactured goods.

Our old friend, Mr. R. A. Naylor, has during the past week been "staggering huntauity,"—to borrow the phrase of a much humbler individual, one Paul Kruger. Last Saturday, he informed the Warrington public through our Conservative contemporary, that" the man who would most certainly be elected if he consented to stand (for Watrington) is none other than your obedient servant, R. A. Naylor." Fancy! Hide your diminished heads, oh ye Pierpoints and Crosfields, ye Greenalls and Parrs, for a greater has come amongst ye. " Most certainly be elected," "none other than," etc., Phew! Bow down and worship, oh ye common people! As far as we can ascertain the common people were more inclined to sneer and wish he really would stand, so that they might testify at the polls their estimate of Mr. Naylor. Unfortunately the pleasure has bean denied them, and fallen to the lot of the ,elactors in Carnarvon Boroughs, where Mr. Naylor was adopted as the Unionist Candidate in opposition to Mr. Lloyd George M.P., on the same day that his remarkable letter was published in Warrington. The delicate duty of appraising Mr. Naylor may, we think, be safely left in the hands of the Carnarvon people. He has commenced his candidature by an ebullition of heroics which culminates with: "We laugh at impossibilities, and cry, it shall be done !" Furthermore Mr. Naylor is said to have, "in the interests of patriotism, refused the offer of a safe seat elsewhere "—pre- sumably Warrington. But what an egregious egotism all this reveals! Mr. Naylor reminds us of Humpty Dumpty, and we are inclined to think that he will share that worthy's fate by crossing swords with Mr. Lloyd George, M.P.

Four hours were rPquired on Tuesday for the discussion of the Town Council business, and some of it had to be again postponed even then. A good deal was accomplished in the time, however. First and foremost the inquiry question was settled by the almost unanimous adoption of the scheme formulated by a special committee of Non-Traders and Corporation representatives, and formally proposed by Councillor Tinnion. The latter explained fully the nature of the negotiations which had led to the drawing-up of such a basis tor inquiry. While it did not absolutely satisfy him it embodied the most reasonable terms they could get from the Non-Traders—and he admitted that in this matter at any rate the Non-Traders had the ratepayers at their backs. This was the proper attitude to adopt and we are glad Coun- cillor Tinnion laid the matter before his fellow- councillors in this light. We have for some time pointed out that it was useless the Council stick- ing out for concessions more or less sentimental, if the Non-Traders were not prepared to grant them on any terms. The primary object was to authorise an inquiry such as would satisfy the ratepayers that there had or had not been trading of an illegal or immoral nature. Councillor Dodds again emphasizad the determination of his com- mittee not to submit to any tampering with the scheme which had been so carefully arranged, and a conciliatory course was also advised by Councillor James Evans and Alderman Bolton, the latter of whom had apparently got over his spleen and was anxious that the inquiry should be held in the interests of ultimate peace. After the speeches of the advocates for an inquiry on the lines proposad,there was practically no fear of any further rejection or adjournment. The Council, almost as a whole, "climbed down" from the pinnacle on which it has stood for so long, and granted the Nun-Traders' request. We are very glad that this course has at last been taken, and we can foresee nothing,in the nature of opposition, which is at all likely to frustrate the inquiry.

Copcillor F. R. Roberts made a big effort to obtain support for his amendments, but, though he delivered the speech of the evening and used same telling arguments, there was no disposition on the part of tne Council to incur any more delay and suspense, and he was defeated. 11119 grounds of his amendments we have previously discussed. Suffice it to say that, primarily, the right of the Non-Traders to appoint on the inquiry commission men wtio have already figured as the accusers was what he most strongly objected to.oa the face of it the objection seems reasonable and in accord with English notions of fairness, but there is one aspect of the contention which was largely ignored. The Commissioners are not to be in any sense judges, so that, in our opinion, the argument re " accuser and judge in one" falls to the ground. We are to have an inguiry, an investigation, not a trial of men who have been charged with trading either illegally or immorally, and it does not appear to us " un-English " that the men who have con- cerned themselves in the agitation should be among the men who have to inquire further into the allegations that have been made. The Com- mission will consist of three nominees from each side, and the fairness of the proceedings ought to, and we think will, be assured.

Now thst the inquiry scheme has been" signed, sealed, and delivered" it would appear that therd is not much difficulty in the way of it being put into operation forthwith. We are somewhat sceptical yet, however, of an inquiry taking place on the lines agreed upon. There is one difficulty which may prove in superable. Clause 4 specifies, as many of our readers will recollect, "that the commissioners shall receive no remuneration." Where, we ask once more, are the Corporation going to find three gentlemen who will undertake such a ;aborious and thAnkless task without reward!? The Non-Traders are eager, and will have little difficulty in appointing their three Commissioners, but op the Corporation side the case is different. Were we asked to be one of the gentlemen conducting an inquiry likely to last for months and bristle with knotty points, we should expect to be well paid for the work, and so will the appointed three in all proba- bility. Clause 4 is more likely to wreck the scheme that the combined exertions of the fiercest opponents of an inquiry,and we shall be interested in seeing how the problem will be solved.

Though so "short and sweet," Councillor Dodds is far from satisfied with the report of the Borough auditors, and on Tuesday he called attention to several matters which he thought might have been noticed by the auditors. The most important was the question of depreciation on the Electic Light and Tramways undertakings. Councillor Dodds dis igrees with the policy of wiping-oil borrowed capital by a sinking fund and maintaining the plant out of revenue, and at the same time allow- ing nothing for depreciation. Apparently his criticism convicts the committee of a neglect of one of the most important elemen- tary business principles, but we are in- clined to think there is " more than meets the eye" in'ithe consideration of the question. The point of view of the Electricity and Tramways Committee is, we believe, that to make three charges on capital,—interest sinking fund charges, and depreciation—would be a mistake, when it is pobsible to pay off the borrowed money and at the same time keep the plant efficient out of revenue. By continuous renewal, doubtless, the working plant could be maintained at such a level as to ob- viate any necessity for a depreciation fund, but ' this does not cover buildings, rails, standards, car- bodies and like properties which it is not possible to renew piecemeal, and which certainly will not improve with keeping. Another objection on the part of the Committee is to let such a sum of money as would be represented by an allowance for depreciation, lie idle. The question is, however, a large one,and it demands fuller elucidation. We expect to he able to publish some instructive matter on the subject next week.

Presumably the last has now been heard of the notorious slack contract. Alderman Francomb's explanation makes it clear how the Warrington Slate Company came to be supplying slack for which Messrs. Richard Evans and Company

===== 0002601_19030815_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
had obtained the contract. The blame still rests with the officials of the Water Com-

mittee, and the explanation of the Chairman only reflects more seriously on the want of inter-communication between the committee and its employees. The latter appear to have done just as they thought fit, which somewhat justifies Councillof Pemberton's assertion that the Corpor- ation was over-ridden by its officials, though we dissent from the comprehensiveness of his condem- nation. Probably the officials of the Water Department are the only ones who would have dared to play tricks with a contract, especially' in these days of criticism and mistrust. There is room for improvement in the business methods of the Water Committee, which in recent years has had the handling ol some huge sums of money, and we hope the opportunity of putting the house in order will at once be taken.

The business of the Warrington Board. of Guardians is never of an exhilarating description and on Wednesday several matters were introduced which were not calculated to raise tne spirits ei the Guardians. In the first place, the Master re- ported that the Workhouse was overcrowded 10 the hospital and female house block and more accommodation must be provided for the winter months. Then an adverse report was presented on the condition of the electrical storage batteries, which have been damaged through neglect to such an extent that 1:300 is neces- sary to put the apparatus in sound work. - ing order. This was a sufficiently bitter • Plll to swallow, but the Clerk presented them with a phial of a like character by drawing a doleful picture of the County finances,in which he showed that while the rateable area was decreasing the expenditure was steadily increasing. He wsured the Guardians that the County Rate call would be an increase upon that of last year, apart altogether from the extra rate which would have to be levied for educational purposes. The Guardians accepted the situation quits philosophically and signed the County rate calls, without even knowing what the County rate is to be. The Clerk was instructed to fill in the amount required when he received the County rate notice. Ratepayers in the Borough have had an unpleasant experience of heavy rates for some time, and it appears that the ratepayers in the country districts are to have a like experience.

The Walton Show is growing in importance This was evidenced on Thursday by the excelleno class of entries and the large attendance of the general public. Those who remember the earlY beginnings of the society with its first show cf mares and foals in the Old Barracks Yard at War- rington must be agreeably surprised ,gt the pro- gress which has been made. The society be° passed through some difficult times since thee and it is possible that if it had not been for the excellent backing which the soctety has received from Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., it would have gone out of existence ere this. Only last yØf Sir Gilbert relieved the Society from its financie' difficulties by wiping off an adverse balance of £3oo' The society was more fortunate in regard to the weather on Thursday and th;s will no doubt reflecS itself in a more satisfactory balance sheet than Wes presented last year. Although the society is e° longer known as a Mare and Foal Society, still the chief feature of the show remains with hors There was an exceptionally fine display of horse° on Thursday and close observers were able to no' tice the steady and solid progress which has bee° made in the breeding of good animals in thie district. The society could hardly be said to hale justified its existence unless an improvement ja the breeding of local stock were the result, but the mere fact that local exhibitors were able t° compete successfully in the open classes is evidence of the all-round improvement which has boa made. This is perhaps hardly to be wondered LO, in a district possessing a stud of the excellence ot Sir Gilbert Greenall'd. In the cattle classes a like improvement will in all probability be noticeable in course of time. Takon as a whole this year'll show must rank as one of the most successful el the series.

ST. HELENS.

ALLEGED THE FROM A CRICKET PAVILION.

At St. Helens, on Tuesday, William Lightfoot, 71, Grafton-street, Thomas Forster,and Alfred Pennington, of Havelock-street, who were charged, with breaking and ,entering the pavilion of the Eccleston Bowling Club, Limited, and taking 190 cigars and a large quantity of spirits, beer, &c., value £7 Os. 70., were committed to the sesssons.

VOLUNTEER ENGIN'EERS IN DISGRACE. A most regrettable and unusual incident marked last Week's camp of the St. Helens Engineers at Morecambe. 'Considerable reticence is being observed in the corps on the matter, but it is stated that a party of three or four men were dismissed from the regiment in disgrace on Thursday for neglect of, and absenting themselves from, guard duty. The disgraced engineers include a sergeant and a corporal. The colonel and officers of the corps hay.° done a great deal during the past five or six years to improve the status and personnel of the regiment,and When the matter cropped up, the colonel unhesitatingly enforced the penalty which follows so serious a breach of discipline.

MR. R. A. NAYLOR AS PARLIA- MENTARY CANDIDATE.

To Oppose Mr. Lloyd George in Carnarvon Boroughs.

ASTONISHING SEQUENCE OF EVENTS.

Last Saturday our Conservative contemporary pub- i shed a

REMA.RKAJ3LE LETTER

?rom Mr. R. A. Naylor, in reply to a correspondent who had suggested that Councillor Dodds entertained Parlia- mentary ambitions. In the course of the letter Mr. Naylor says : "I feel it therefore my duty to assure your corres- pondent that he is quite mistaken as to Mr. Dodds' aspirations, for instead of this gentleman desiring to represent Warrington in the next Parliament I know for a fact that he has used all his persuasive eloquence to induce a man, who thinks not as he thinks politically, to undertake the position of candidate for the Non-Traders at the coming election. Probably Mr. Dodds will not thank me for removing a wrong impression, for he is quite able to fight his own battles. As" Conservative "and those who have read his letter may wish to know who the suggested Candi- date is, I beg to say that in order to clear Mr Dodds in their eyes from the imputations so recklessly hurled at him, the man who would most certainly b elected if he consented to stand is none other than Your Obedient Servant.

R. A. NAYLOR. St. Michael's House, Egerton Gardens, London S. W. August 4th 1903. The italics are, of course, ours. Needless to say this concluding sentence created an astounding sensation in the town, which had never looked upon the writer as a possible Candidate under any circumstances.

===== 0002601_19030815_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
ADOPTED AT CARNARVON.

On Monday appeared the announcement in the• Press that Mr. R, A. Naylor had been adopted as Unionist candidate for Carnarvon Boroughs in opposition to Mr. Lloyd George, M.P. At the Conservative Club, Carnarvon, on Saturday Me Naylor delivered an address to his prospective consti- tuents in which he said: "He was coming into the constituency with the intention of "speaking the truth," and he would not lower himself to deception, even if he knew he would gain the seat thereby. He then dealt "very eloquently" with the education and fiscal ques- tions. Dealing with the latter he quoted from his own work upon the subject, written ten years ago, in which he predicted the present position of affairs. He re- gretted to say that the statistics of our trading relations with foreign countries were worse now than they were then, and it appeared to him that if the state of things continued in the same direction the working classes of this country would not have to talk about cheap bread or dear bread, but would have to cry for the money to buy any bread at all If the working classes of the country once thoroughl understood the danger that existed of losing much o the trade enjoyed at the present moment they would rise up as one man, and in the interests of themselves and families declare that the Government should take some means of protecting theit liv el ihood. Mr. Naylor, in concluding his address, asked the delegates to do their utmost for the Unionist cause in the constituency, and, although there were great difficulties to face, not to be discouraged, but to be animated by the sentiments of one of the poets who said, "WE LAUGH AT IMPOSSIBILITIES, AND CRY IT SHALL (Loud cheers).

===== 0002601_19030815_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
BE DONE."

- The" North Wales Observer and Express,"commenting on the above speech says: "There is an element of heroism about the prophetic utterance at the conclusion of the speech at Carnarvon on Saturday. "We laugh at impossibilities, and cry, 'lt shall be done!" There is something akin to the sublime in that impressive cry. When it is remembered that the "it" referred to stands for the ousting of Mr. Lloyd-George from the representation of the Carnarvon boroughs the magnifi- cence of the courage implied may be understood. Mr. Naylor deserves a better fate than to be rudely sub- jected by his Tory friends to the buffets of a political campaign. The temperament that is so essentially the seer's and the hymnwriter's is not adapted for the war- fare generally connected with Carnarvon borough elec- tions."

Finally, Mr. Naylor is said "in the interests of patriotism', to have

• REFUSED A SAFE SEAT elsewhere in response to the call to do battle against Mr. Lloyd George.

Letter to Councillor Dodds. "The Dictates of a Lofty Patriotism."

The following is the copy of an explanatory letter Mr. Naylor has addressed to Councillor Dodds : "St. Michael's House, Egerton Gardens, London, S.W., August 12, 1903. "Dear Mr. Dodds,—Yonr kind letter of the 10th, and I can quite see my letter in the 'Warrington Observer' of Saturday last may, in addition to the impression I I desired, have placed you in an unpleasant position in regard to the particular political party with which your name has been honourablyassocialed. I therefore have great pleasure in stating, as you desire, that had I con- sented to accept the generous invitation of the Non• Traders to become their Parliamentary Candidate it would have heen asan opponent of all questionable deal- ings of all Corporate bodies and excessive local and national expenditure, and not as the nominee of any political party. My conversation with you was perfectly clear, i.e.. if' I were under no obligation to any other constituency and I was officially approached by your Association in the above connection, I should be glad to consider such a proposition. Now, as you suggest my acceptance as candidate for tho Carnarvon Boroughs in conjunction with the letter referred to may have given the impres- oion you state. I beg to say most emphatically that had I contested Warrington on the lines indicated, I should have respected the feelings of those who think differently to what I do upon the various questions now before the country, I am just as muoh opposed to our fiscal system as I am to that of extrayagauce with the public money, and as the former is one of my specialities I have been glad to respond to the cry from afar and I intend to fight the Carnarvon Boroughs upon this question, not in the interests of a party,but in response to the dictates of a lofty patriotism which places the well being of the great mass of people before any other consideration. You are quite at liberty to make this letter public, aud I hope it will serve the purposes desired. With every good wish, yours faithfully, R. A. NAYLOR.

Death of Mrs. Hickman. We regret to announce that Mrs. Hickman, wife of Mr. William Hickman, auctioneer, 13, Arpley street, passed away at her residence on Wednesday. The deCeased lady was held in high esteem by a large circle of friends and the news of her death was received with deep regret. Much sympathy is felt with Mr. Hickman and the members of his family in their bereavement. The funeral will take place at the Warrington Cemetery ernoon). There will be a service this (Saturday aftlevaa Church at 1.45.

ASK for CUNNDIGHAM'S HIGII-CLA.SS MINERA WATERS.—Advt. The "Countess of Stamford" Again. The Macclesfield county magistrates on Tuesday had to submit to a lengthy story from Agnes G-ilroy, des- cribed as the Countess of Stamford and Warrington. , Mrs. Gilroy, with her husband, made an application for a summons against William Harris, Detective Superintendent of the North Staffordshire Railway Company, whom .she charged with perjury. She also applied for a summons against the Congleton Police. for illegal arrest. In her statement 'she said she was convicted of attempting to defraud the railway com- pany on the 28th March, an accusation she denied. She disputed that the summons had been rightly served as it was put under the door of an empty house at Congleton whilst she was at Warrington. She was afterwards arrested at Congleton and £2 6s. taken from her. This, she said, was nothing but abomin- able extortion and blackmail. The magistrates left the Court, the countess continuing to .tell her tale of woe to the pollee. BEST BOOTS ion ALL.—UNDER the CLOCK.—Advt. Disastrous Fire at Warrington. There was a disastrous fire early on Wednesday morning, the Britannia Soap Works, Knutsford-road, being almost completely gutted. Constable Gilchrist discovered the outbreak at 1.30 and immediately gave the alarm. The fire brigade, under the command of Superintendent and Deputy Superintendent Frank Turner, promptly turned out with the- steam engine Major, and on their arrival they found the building was in full blaze. The works were constructed entirely of corrugated iron and wood and a portion had already fallen in. The efforts of the brigade were largely devoted to saving the boiling shed and the mixing room, and though some damage was caused by water these departments were saved. -The other rooms, however, including the perfume room, the store-room, the paper and box-rooms, the small dry- room, and a portion of the milling-room were com pletely gutted. How the fire originated is a mystery as the place was apparently quite safe about midnight. The damage is estimated at between £3,000 and S:4,000.

There was an interesting presentation at the monthly meeting of the Garibaldi Lodge of the National Independent Order of Oddfellows held on Saturday, the retiring Grand-master,—Bro. Richard Blackshaw being the recipient of an engraved P.O-.'s jewel for services rendered as an officer. P.P.G. Mr. Smith]made the presentation and said he could safely say without fear of contradiction that they never had a more worthy member than Bro. Blackshaw as he had attended to his duty very satisfactorily and without a single complaint. (Applause.) Secretary (Bro. Dickenson) and P.G. J. Hughes endorsed the remarks of the previous speaker. Bro. Blackshaw, in accept- ing the present., said it had been his ambition for about three years to possess a jewel, and he was very pleased he had reached that ambition. Still its possession would not deter him from still plodding on in the interests of Oddfellowship.

MR. PIERPOINT AND CYPRUS.

Chilling Reply from Mr. Chamberlain.

In the House of Commons on Monday night, in the discussion on the vote for the diplomatic service, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P. for Warrington,called attention to the affairslof Cyprus. He stated that the grant in aid this year, instead of being of the average, some £30,000 was £87,000. That was absolutely necessary, because the island had gone through a drought such as it had not experienced since 1873. Cyprus paid regularly every year on the average £60,000, which was spent elsewhere. He did not deny that Cyprus had benefited very largely from our occupa- tion of the island, but his great desire was that the island should benefit a great deal more, and have the I enjoyment of its own taxation. He pressed on the , Colonial Secretary the importance of considering the interests of the people as to the tribute. It was inevit- able that the people shauld become discontented when they had to pay such a large amount of money, especi- ally in view of the fact that they were far from being a rich people. We had given them the benefits of the administration of this country. We ought also to offer them tke beneficence and the justice of this country. Mr. Chamberlain, in reply, congratulated his hon, friend on the perseverance with which he reverted to the question of the Cyprus tribute. He thought that since he had held his present office the hon. gentleman had made the same speech every year, and he had made the same speech in reply to his hon. friend ; but he was sorry to say that they had not got much further. He had explained more than once that when we, took possession of Cyprus there was this moirt- gage of about £90,000 a year upon the resources of the island, and that if we had not taken pos- session the island would not have obtained any reduction of the trioute, Whereas it now received annually a grant in aid of £30,000. He did not consider that the tribute, as applied to the payment of the guaranteed Turkish loan of 1855, had been a saving to this country or to France, and he denied that if the tribute had not been so applied either France or Great Britain would have treated the loan as a bad debt; they would have laid claim to other Turkish resources. This country, he proceeded to say, would have been richer if it had not taken possession of Cyprus. From our rule the islanders had derived enormous benefits. He could hold out no hope of a change in respect of the tribute. Before resuming his seat the right hon. gentleman gave some account of the irrigation and development works undertaken in the island for the advantage of the in- habitants.

===== 0002601_19030815_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WARRINGTON VOLUNTEERS. Close of the Camp. A SCOUT REWARDED.

The local Volunteers brought their annual ca.-up at Lewaigue, 1.0. M. ton close lastweek. On Saturday a "falling in" competition was arranged between companies. The men had to be inside the r tents when the bugle sounded.and then form up in their own lines and march on to the parade ground. Prizes were also offered for the cleanest tents. The rest of the day was spent in preparing for home. On Sunday morning, reveille sounded at 5 a.m. and tents were struck after breakfast. At 9 o'clock the battalion paraded for the return home. Before leaving camp, Lieut-Colonel Edelsten, the new commanding officer, after complimenting the battalion on its be- haviour distributed the following prizes :—For the smartest turn-out" H" Company (Captain It. Smith) ; cleanest tent in the battalion, Ist prize, No:7 tent "F " Company ; 2nd, No. 5 tent "0" (Newton) Company. There was also a prize for the cleanest tent in each line. Colonel Edelsten then made a few remarks and said he wished to reward one man in particular for his good work as a scout during camp. The scouting of the battalion had very much improved this year and Captain B. Fairclough who commanded the scouts had spoken very favourably of Private Morgan of " D "Company. This man was then called out of the ranks, presented with 10s., and promoted to the position of lance- corporal.

The men marched out of camp, wearing—owing to the rain which was falling,—their great coats.At about noon they sailed in the Empress Queen for the Prince's Land- ing Stage, Liverpool, which was reached at 5.30. The passage was an exceedingly rough one, and most of the men suffered from mal de mer, and ware also saturated with rain and waves. To use the words of one of the sufferers, the sight on board was "really pitiable." No wonder, therefore, that the travellers one and all were very glad indeed to find themselves on terra firma.

Th; Volunteers marched across Liverpool to the Central Station and entrained, the first batch at a quarter past six, and the second batch a few minutes later The scone from the• carriage windows on arrival at Warrington at about 7.15 was an extraordinary one. The one question of the returning men was "How are we going to march through the crowd ?" However a clear way was made for them and to the strains of the band, the men wended their way to the Drill Hall. Here they were dismissed, one and all glad to get home, but not, as is rumoured in some quarters, dissatisfied with their experiences as citizen soldiers. _ It is satisfactory to learn that the local men left an exceedingly good impression behind them amongst the inhabitants and visitors of Ma,uxland. This is saying a great deal but the compliment has been well deserved.

LOCAL SHEPARDRY.

Burial Society Changes :its Nalll3.

The members of the Warrington Burial Society of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds assembled at the British Workman Public House, Horsemarket-street on Monday, the occasion being the 32nd anniversary of the establishment of the Society. The members attended in good force, news having reached them that it would probably be the last time they would meet under the title they have held for over 32 years, The meeting was presided over by the President- (Mr. Joseph Williams) and he was supported by the Vice-president (Mr. Joseph Adams), treasurer, James Parr; trustees, William Holmes, Peter Edgar, William ITlckersley; auditor, John Jones and the General Secretary (Mr. Lawrence Weir) and others.

The CEIAIRMAN. in his. opening remarks, referred to the progress the Societyshad made since he had known it as a collecting society. They had experienced thirty- two years of complete success. Looking at the past history of the Society it was marvellous to note the stand it had taken in the competition with the other collecting societies, and the confidence it had gained for itself from the outsidepublic. The balance sheet,which was about to be presented, was full of hope and encouragement to all concerned. The numerical condi- tion of the Society was an that could be desired, as every year brought fresh gains, and he hoped that the deliberation of that night would bring even a better result in the future.

The GENERAL SECRETARY was then called upon to submit his annual report which showed that the total worth of the Burial Society amounted to £2,601 115.4 d. compared with £2,470 13s. 9d. for the previous year, a gain of £l9O 175.7 d., and the numerical condition was 1331, compared with 1291 last year. In conclusion the Secretary urged upon the meeting the advisability of taking into consideration the lengthened periods of membership in the future. He said members deserved some recognition after they had loyally been with them from the formatiom of the Society, and if possible he thought they should give better benefits to its ordinary members Bro. Joseph Adams was elected President and Bro. Joseph Ashto vice-president fur the year,and a per- manent committee consisting of Messrs. John Jones, William Slevin, Thomas Miller, William Hall. John Haloy,David Harris, James Grealy. W. and O's are Frederick Massey, Henry Cook, Samuel Bradshaw, Henry Warburton.

A very lengthy agenda was gone through and the most important and interesting motion was the one asking the members to change their trading name from the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds •to the Warrington Burial Society This, it is stated, has been brought about through the over-bearing conduct of the officials in connection with the order. Aunanimous vote was given in favour of the motion, and the votes of thanks concluded the pro- ceedings.

IVIR. W. P. RYLANDS' REPLY TO MR.

CROSFIELD.

The Case for Preferential Tariffs.

Alft. W. PETER RYLANDS writes :

It is one of the first principles of economics that the price of a commodity depends upon its scarcity or plentifulness in relation to its demand. Gold is as much a commodity as iron or wheat, and it is a purely arbitrary convention that gold has been selected as a universal medium of exchange. The value or price of gold measured say in wheat will therefore vary for exactly the same causes, and in precisely the same manner as the value or price of anything else. The effect cannot, of course, be detected in the price of gold measured in sovereigns, and in consequence it is not so obvious. None the less, variations do take place and are reflected by a general rise or fall in the prices of commodities measured in sovereigns. The value or purchasing power of gold will therefore like every other commodity depend upon the plentifulness or scarcity of the supply, or in other words upon the ease or diffi- culty with which it is obtained. If gold is obtained or earned easily by the members of a community, the pur- chasing power of a sovereign within that community will be low, and if wages and profits are, low, the purchasing power of a sovereign will be correspondingly increased.

In a previous letter I gave as a familiar instance of this phenomenonthealluvial gold diggings in Australia, lor the early days of the Kimberley Diamond Mines, when the ease with which gold was obtained was reflec- ted in the enormous price which had to be paid for everything. This proposition is important because so much nonsense is talked about the advantage of cheap- ness, arid about the purchasing power of a sovereign, 1 that it is well that anyone desiring to understand the difficult questions of political economy should realise at the outset thatthe cost of necessaries of life is merely relative, and the fact that the cost may be advanced as alresult of an alteration in the Fiscal Policy of a nation is notnecessarily an evil. A capital illustration of this was given by Mr. H. P. Hughes in your issue of the 25th July comparing the saving power of an American with that of an English workman, much to the disadvan- tage of the latter, and also the instructive case of the amazed Irishman when asked is. per dozen for eggs in England, which in Ireland he had been accustomed to buy for 6d. I have amplified this proposition somewhat at length, as Mr. Crostield still appears to have some difficulty on the point, or is it the advocate's objection to admitting anything ?

The effect of a small import duty upon foodstuffs upon the cost of living in relation to wages is one which cannot be absolutely predicated. I think, how- ever, it, is certain that a small import duty upon food- stuffs imported from countries other than our Colonies will have a very small effect, if any, upon the selling price. The wheat growing area within the British Empire is enormous, and the competition among duty free wheat would be quite keen enough to prevent the price appreciably advancing, and the Americans, in a manner quite familiar to manufacturers exporting to a protected country, would pay the greater part of the duty themselves. Ido not admit for an instant that the cost of Living would be increased at all, but in any case lam confident the increased cost would not be more than a very small proportion of the duty. This I understand is exactly the position taken up by Mr. Chamberlain, and is in accord with all experience, and common sense. The revenues derived from American and Russian foodstuffs would be employed in remitting taxation, for instance, on tea or tobacco, or available in any other way in the interest of the community. Any small increase in the cost of living might well be counter-balanced by reduced taxation in other directions, or if a suitable scheme could be devised, by a system of Old Age Pensions to the advantage of the working classes of this country. While therefore I believe the cost of living would be practically unaffected, and any revenue from taxed food would ben clear gain to the community, I am satisfied that even if the cost of living were increased, it would be more than counter-balanced by savings in otherres- pects.

It is impossible to dogmatize as to the effect of this policy upon wages. Han alteration in our fiscal policy results in increased demands upon our manufacturers, for which reason alone any alteration will be adopted the workingmen will derive the advantage. By far the largest proportion of the selling price of a commodity is wages, and if several millions per linnum are distri- buted among the working classes, lam sure they will notstop to discuss whether it is accomplished by means of anadvance, or of an increase in wages. It is an old saying that figures can be made to !prove anything, and Mr. Crostield appears to be a past master in this art. In your issue of July 11th he produced figures to show that we had 71 per cent of the total imports into Australia, whereas the official returns show, as I pointed out in my last letter our exports to Australia in 1900 were 211 millions out of a total import of 70 millions, or but a trifle more than 25 per cent. I then pointed out that the import trade of the Colonies amounted to 250 millions sterling per annum, of which 100 millions only came from this country.—Mr. Crosfield's next endeavour was to whittle away the importance of this 150 millions of trade at present secured by our foreign competitors. Ha com- mences by sweeping away India. Why on earth he should object to the inclusion of India I cannot imagine. India is our most important possession, with the largest population, and the greatest demand for our manu- factures. Any policy which is good for the Colonies will be good forllndia, and I should not have thought that it would have occurred to anybody that such an important factor as India should be omitted from the present consideration. In this way Mr. Crosfield reduces the 150 millions of available trade down to 50 millions, and then proceeds to show that the 50 millions is more than half raw materials and goods that we could not supply, and by means of some ingenious cal- culations he reduces the trade that we could possibly touch to about 10 millions sterling Mr. Crosfield's argument is ingenuous if not ingenious- and I must demur altogether to such special pleading. Of course, this country could not secure the whole of the 150 millions of trade. I have tbe details of the Imports into the Colonies before me. and while I can- not find much that can be properly styled raw material, there certainly is a cersidemble quantity of foodstuffs, etc.. which no doubt muct under any circumstances be drawn from the present source of supply. Making every allowance, howdver, there is undoubtedly a very large amount of trade indeed which we could handle, and I have little doubt my suggestion that such additional trade might even. reach 50 millions sterling would not be far short of the mark. It must however be borne in mind that in addition to the extra trade secured by this country, better prices would be obtained in re- spect of a large portion of the 100 millions of Colonial trade which we do at present, making the nett gain much larger than would appear at first sight, and further that the Colonial trade is continually expanding, and the British Colonies are unquestionably the great , markets of the future. By preserving the Colonial markets to ourselves it will be possible for us to hold , our own in future, in spite of the fact that the protec- tive policy of our competitors is rapidly closing to us the doors of the other markets of the World. In addition to these advantages to this country both immediate and prospective, theadvantage of developing' the wheat growing area of the Empire can hardly be under-estimated. Our dependence for food upon the good-will of certain foreign countries is regarded by many with apprehension in view of contingencies, the possibility of which must always be tome in mind. Further the world is rapidly getting filled up, and the day must inevitably come when there will be a difficulty in growing sufficient wheat for its population. To pro- tect our people against the pressure of that time it is only prudent to link our Colonies more closely to us and to develop the wheat growing area under our control.

This, Sir, I apprehend to be the CIS3 for Prefer- ential Tariffs,mad I submit the case is a strong one. The policy of Preferential Tariffs is the only policy which hae,.:as3yet, been put forward for our consideration. The details of carrying out this policy have yet to be ascer- tained, and I deprecate the method so much adoptei by the opponents to.this policy of raising all sorts of ob- jections and .difficulties in the way of carrying such a policy into effect in order to burke discussion of the main principle involved. As to the necessity of some policy to save the industries of this country I believe there is little question. When those responsible have elaborated the details of a scheme, it will be time for us to examine them in all their bearings. It is un- English to be afraid of difficulties, and although the difficulties will undoubtedly be great, I am confident that if it is the desire of the members of our Empire to arrive at some system whereby Imperial trade may be preserved as far as possible for Imperial Manufacturers, some satisfactory method of carrying such a policy into effect will be devised.

Before leaving the subject there are one or two specific points in Mr. Crosfleld's letter upon which I should like to write a few words. I was extremely sorry to see Mr. Crosfield quote at length from MY. Andrew Carnegie's unfortunate letter to the "Times.' That letter, a mixture of threats and entreaty, was couched in terms not at all calculated to fall sympa- thetically on British ears. Appeals of this kind come ill from a man whose enormous fortune has been built up by heavy protective tariffs in his country, sand by the practice of methods most hostile to Britain's trade, while threats could only have an effect quite contrary to the intention of their user. Much in Mr. Carnegie's letter, however, is entirely without foundation. The shipthents from Caaada through American ports depend upon treaties with America, the benefits of which to the

- latter are quite as great or greater than any derived by Canada. There is no doubt that the Americans would not dream of taking such an extreme course as that sug- gested by Mr. Carnegie for fear of consequences far too serious for them to contemplate. I am pleased to note that Mr. Crosfield seems to think that the conditions of the export iron trade of this country- require some special • consideration, and I welcome his agreement if only to that extent. He, however, follows his admission by statistics and figures which, to say the least are most misleading, although I do not suggest that Mr. Crosfield deliberately used them for this purpose. The profits of iron works cannot be de-severed from the profits of collieries. Most of the large iron companies in this country are coal owners. I have no doubt, therefore, that the figures heigives are the profits resulting from both iron and coal. It is familiar to all that in 1900 there•was a great boon in coal, with the result that coal owners after passing through many years during which they received little, if any, interest upon their capital, were able to secure about 10 or 12 per cent. The result of this was to inflate the profits from 1998 to 1900, and quite accounts for the increase shown in Mr. Crosfleld's figures. To select four firms such as the Consett Co. Caramel's, Vickers and J. Brown and Co., as typical of Iron Companies as a whole is quite unjustifiable. The Consett Iron Company own the best and easiest worked coal in the world, and are able to mine their coal at about half the cost of mining anywhere else. This special circumstance enables them to make profits on quite a different scale to any of their competitors Cammells, Vickers and Brown and Co., are makers of armour plates for warships. This is their chief busi- ness, and in this they entirely escape from the exigen- cies of foreign compatition. Firms of this kind cannot be. regarded in any sense of the word as representative of the iron trade in this country. I think if Mr. Cros- field will ask any iron manufacturer who has to deal with the ordinary iron trade, and meet the com- petitions of the Americans and Germans, he will hear a very different story. The largest iron and steel companies in this country of the type I have just mentioned have the greatest difficulty in holding their own, and only by the practice of the most rigid economy are they able to keep their figures on the right side. A well-known company in the North of England, one of the largest, best equipped and best managed firms In this country, within the last two years declared a substantial loss as the result of a year's work- ing. The tendency seems always for the worse, each depression is more severe than the one that preceded it, and while I do not admit that the English manu- facturer is deficient in enterprise, ability or determina- tion, I maintain that it is the act of a fool to shut the stable door after the horse has been stolen, and of a wise man to take time by the forelock, and set his house in order against the storms which the future inevitably has in store.

===== 0002601_19030815_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
SHERLOCK HOLMES AND THE MYSTERY OF THE MOTOR CAR.

Mr. C. E. PARKER. writes :

Sherlock Holmes (Sir Conan Doyle) has added to hi, widely known writings a mystery which appearha ti his own elucidation. The man who has unravelled for us so many tangled skeins, appears absolutely befogged in face of the mystery of the motor car.

A few weeks back in the" Spectator "he raised the difficulty, which was something like this—if I buy a motor car in France for £l,OOO does not my action re- sult in a less to British Capital and Labour of £1,000? The Editor of the" Spectator "who by the way is doing splendid service in the Free Spectator," cause, pointed out that this was not so inasmuch as the £l,OOO paid by Sir Conan Doyle to the French maker was paid. for not in gold but in English goods. That all trade was based on exchange and that money merely facilitated exchange. But our literary knight carmotsee this at all and to state his case in his own words :

"I am aware that it has been received as an axiom that trade begets trade, but that seems to me to be the very theory whioli is now upon its trial. Your correspondents both take the view'that my .£l,OOO is not lost to Great Britain, but that it returns in payment of British products. The benefit of a concrete case like this is that one might make an attempt to trace approximately the channels through which the money continues to flow. M. Panhard (the French motor maker) for example, pays in my cheque, and transfers my money eventually to his banking account. Some proportion of it is expended in the upkeep of his factory, which becomes a valuable taxable asset for the French government. The balance of the money is divided between the employers and the work- people. In the expenditure of the employers certain a rtic!es of British manufacture may have a place. In that of the workman, with his blue blouse, his via ordinaire, his sabots, and his own French outfit, there is very little that could come back to us. He saves some of the money, and it may go to the next Russian loan. Surely it would be a fair state- ment to say that of that £l,OOO not £2O would ever filter back to England. In the other case the whole £l,OOO is being devotei to the employment of our own people. Surely it is better to retain that certain £l,OOO within the country than to send it forth on the chance of some small proportion of it coming back in trade. It may be fairlyargned that this is a special case, and that the balance of trade asserts itself in larger transactions. We buy from America £100,000,000 worth of goods, and return £18,000,000. Where is the balance ?"

Thus we see the plot thickens, mystery is added to mystery. Where is the balance? What has become of this odd £82,000,000? Surely of all his "cases" the unearthing of this little difference should not baffle the power of -this detective king, and he should not find it a difficult task to discover the path of this hidden treasure.

But to return to the Mot % Car. One thousand pounds is paid to the French maker" who pays in m , cheque and transfers my money eventually to his bank- ing account" and then we are told what this money supplied by the banker does. But where it seems to me ISir Conan Doyle misses the point, and goes off on a i false scent, is n thinking that it is his actual money that goes to the French maker. The cheque is not money it is an instruction to some London banker to pay a IFrench motor maker £l,OOO in English coin or notes. lint the Frenchman does not want English money, could do nothing with it if he had it4-except to get rid of it for something more useful. The Frenchman takes his cheque to the banker, who will advance in French money the equivalent of one thou- sand English pounds,. What then does the banker do? He takes the draft, cheque, or bill, ' to the Clearing House' enters into communication with English banks who on their side have collected cheques bills, drafts, &c., upon Paris and the totals are balanced or "cleared." The balance, on either side, may be squared in bullion, or dealt with some other way. We see therefore that the money is only a means of facili- tating exchange, and that the motor car is really exchanged for something that English capital and labour has made, it is the other side of the exchange, a side which, by the way, most of your correspondents persistently ignore. But there is the question of the Balance of Trade which troubles Sir Conan Doyle. Suppose that instead of one a thousand English motorists buy French motor cars, we should then soon have in Paris an accumulation of bills, drafts, and cheques drawn on London, the re- sult would be that the balance against England would have to be liquidated in some way. At first bullion might play some part, but this would soon result in an accumulation of English gold, and its equivalent, in Paris, far in excess of ordinary requirements. The rate of exchange would be affected. In consequence of the abundance, the relation between the sovereign and the' French money would be altered, and if 25 francs were the exchange value in ordinary times, if the movement , were pronounced, e4sovereigni and everything that a sovereign is the measure of, might be exchanged for say 24 francs. Here then would be the Frencbbuyeef chance. If English goods measured by a sovereign, for which formerly he was paying 25 francs, can now be bought for 21 francs he gets in addition to his ordinary profit, the profit arising out of the difference of exchange, the result of Englishmen purchas- ing French motor cars. Thus imports beget ex- ports. A 'further consequence of the glut of gold in Paris, the Bank rate would be reduced, making money more plentiful and cheaper,and this would enable French buyers to hold stocks at less cost, and make them more disposed to buy. As soon as the French begin to buy, the accumulation of English gold, drafts and bills, which have been in excess in Paris begin to flow back to England in payment of the French pur chases, made additionally profitable through the differ ence of exchange.

So far we have considered the question from the French standpoint. Let us now look at the question from the English side. Here the position would be exactly reversed. In consequence of the drain of gold, the Bank of England would put up the rate to protect its reserve, and money would become dearer.lt would be more expensive to hold stocks and the tendency would be to realize. Naturally merchants and manufac- turers would seek their French customers who on account of the difference of rate of exchange could profitably buy, and the exchange of goods which we call trade thus aided by the position of money would right itself. Goods going to France and being settled for by the bills &c, waiting exchange, and the accumulated gold flowing back to England until normal conditions were once more restored. Thus we see how the ?voter cars which we supposed to have been bought in France would compel France to take our goods in exchange.We have consilered the question only as between the two countries France and England. The same principles hold good, however many countries may be trading together. But the greater number of countries trading together the less violent becomes the difference of exchange. In the case named above, the motor car or whatever causes the balance of trade, might be settled not directly between Bngland and France, but indirectly by a third country. England exports in excess to Turkey, Turkey may pay the excess to France and thus help to liquidate her obligation to England by paying off part of England's obligation to France. It is surprising how little bullion actually passes from one country to another, and the transfer of coin is by no means always to settle differences. If

===== 0002601_19030815_art0072.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====


===== 0002601_19030815_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
every country in the world tried to exclude our goods and still continue to sell to us without buying they would find.the task utterly impossible, as the difference of exchange would enable English goods to climb all barriers.

P:S.—Since writing the above I have had sent to me an admirable pamphlet by Mr. Russell Rea M.P., part of which bears out what I have tried to make clear that I trust you. will find room for the following extract : Some years age when a period of bad trade was coming upon us, exports and employments were declining, but imports were still increasing I remarked to a friend, a large importer of provisions, that, unless things improved, imports would be checked. Being a practical man only, he ridiculed the idea, and said that he could always buy cheeses in Canada and sell them in Liverpool, whether I could export steel rails, or earn freights or not. A few days later the financial machine which holds the balance of international commerce began to work. Excessive payments being due to America,my friend found the exchange going against him • the sovereign for which he sold his cheese would not buy so many dollars and cents to pay for it, his profits began to be gut. Then the machine turned another screw,the exchange reached a point at which it was profitable to send out gold- for if we overrun the constable Mr. Seddon's gold sovereigns begin to go. Then, of course, the bank acted, the reserve had to be protected; up went the rate from 3to 4, 5 and 6 per cent. My. importing friend's profits had now almost gone.. Then dear

money forced weak holders to sell, and down v-vsen.l

them:ice of cheese in Liverpool. My friend iredn-c-e-d. • his shipments as much as possible, but lost much money." Thus the great machine works and keeps the interna- tional balance true.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

There have been so many draws as the resnit of the Warrington eleven's engagements that one really tires of chronicling them. There was another addition to the list on Saturday when the Warringtonians journeyed to Cheadle Huline. Batting first the visitors were well dismissed for 139 and when stumps were drawn Cheadle Hultne had compiled 47 for the loss of 2 wickets.

One party of Warrington players ought to be thankful that they were able to take part in the match at all. The conveyance in which they should have travelled to Cheadle Hulme collapsed on starting, and the occupants were, of course, thrown out. The cap- tain (Mr. Bibby) sustained several bruises but fortun- ately no one was seriously hurt, although all- ex- perienced a shaking. ,

In regard to the individual play the Warrington captain °deserves a full measure ,of praise for his highly creditable batting. He stepped in just at the critical moment and compiled 51 in faultless style. His best strokes included six fours.

In the batting department Teather did good service for the home team, capturing 7 wickets for 39 runs. For Warring,ton,Lynes took 2 wickets at a cost of 19- runs.

The second eleven had an easy task in defeating Cheadle Hulme second contingent, the majority in their favour being 78. The feature of the Warrington innings was H. Bennett's contribution of 48. W. M. Silcock and W. Bennett bowled with deadly effect Silcock capturing 4 wickets for 31 runs and Bennett a like number for 25 runs.

Sankey Wire Mills just mana,gad to get the best o the enciunter with Platt Bridge P.M., the margin being only 6 runs. Evans, who scored 12 not out, for Platt Bridge, was the only batsman to attain double figures.

Latchford Baptists gained a creditable victory over Grappenhall 2nd, the respective totals being 7-6 and. 48. J. Bibby (20) was the most useful batsman, and Conway was to the fore in the batting department, taking 5 wickets for 10 runs.

Cycling.

On Saturday the weather was dull and threatening and only seven members took part in the Warrington Cycling Club's run to Delamere. Those who ven- tured out had a very enjoyable time. On Tuesday the weather was again anything but favourable but eleven members rode t 3 G-lazebury and spent a pleasant evening.. To-day the members will entertain the President at nester.

Angling.

The Association contest, which takes place at Thel- wall to-day is expected to be a record event, and given fine weather there will be keen competition for the good list of prizes offered. Members should bear in mind thatouly those clear on the books will be allowed to compete or receive a prize. No entry from local -nembers will be received on the day of the contest.

It has been decided to take part in the Lifeboat Saturday Demonstration on September sth, when all the specimen fish that can be collected will be exhibited. Will members having any tackle to spare (both a:-..cient and modern) kindly forward it to the Secretary of the Association as early as possible so that they can be properly catalogued? Rods from 9to 11 ft. long will be preferred, and baskets, panniers, landing nets, gaffe. &c. are also required. Pike reds shouid be equipped with spinners.

The River Dee has yielded poor sport, only a few captures being reported- The Gowy at Tottenhall-road has proved a blank. The rumours of pollution a few weeks ago have evidently had some foundation. From Rossett and Llanymynech no reports have reached me.

The Bridgewater Canal maintains its good reputation this season.

Members are warned not to fish that part of the canal between Hough's Bridge and Halton Bridge, as com- plaints havd beed received from that quarter.

The Latchford .Angling Society fished their contest at Barrows Lock, Northwich, on the Bth inst. Fair sport was met with the winner, Mr. 0. Taylor, scaling 11b. 13drms. He was followed somewhat closely by Messrs. T. Chorley, W. W. Starkey, A. Holland, H. Craven, J. Owen. H. Green, H. Thornton, J. Alcock, The party, to the number or 28, drove in conveyances to the scene of operations, all taking part in the contest,. and-though the day was somewhat boisterous the out- ing was thoroughly enjoyed. The whole of the arrange- ments were carried out by Messrs. A. and I. Starkey, H. Green, and W. T. Crank, and complimentary remarks have been made of the order that characterized the whole of the proceedings. The prizes, to the value of £3 10s. will be distributed in due course.

The committee of the association regret to have to record the resignation of Mr. F. J. Emson as assistant secretary, and have to announce the appointment of M. W. Reddish, of 5, Belmont Avenue, Latchford, to succeed Mr. Emson. Mr. Reddish hopes that the same cordial feeling Mr. Emson has received from members of the Association, and from local anglers generally, may be extended to him.

The King Edward the VII. Angling Society fish their match at Vale Royal this week end.

The Committee of the Warrington Workingmen's Angling Association met at the Gladstone Arms lon, Church-street, on the 11th inst. when arrangements were made for the president's match at Waverton. It is re- quested that as many as possible will compete, The draw will take place as Mill Bridge at 4 o'clock a.m. Entries may be made at headquarters up to 9.30 p.m on the 15th.

Broken Cross will be the scene of operations for the Mersey Hotel Contingent next week end, and members are reminded to pay all dues and demands not later than. the 12nd inst.

The association Championship contest for the hand- some Gold,Medal presented by their esteemed chairman. will take place at Agden Bridge on the 22nd inst. Other valuable prizes will be offered and it should be borne in mind that only the 1.15 p.m. train from Arpley will be available for the match.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE

- - It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly oeneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, Injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of .the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the prevalent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adapted to the needs of the mer- chant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a preparation a contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, the Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine ' has failed to give rerief, and we may say further, that it has proved permanently beneficial, when other pre- parations at best only gave temporary relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side, Giddiness, Loss of Appe- tite, also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleep- lessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness; either after an illness, long confinement to ill-ventilated rooms or any other cause. It strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts as the system which have been wea.kesed by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it isa Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease. If you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by taking now a course of this excellent Tonic, which is sold verywhere in bottles at 2s. 9d. and 4s. 6d. each, but would any difficulty be experienced in procuring it, the proprietors • will forward same for the above prices. carriage free. Avoid Imitations. Whenpurchasing,see the name, " Gwilyna Evans," on Label,Stamp, and Bottle Sole Proprietors: Quinine Bitters Manufacturing Com- pany Limited, Llanelly, South Wales.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A MISERABLE WARRINGTON 11011 E.—A CHILD'S SHOCKING CONDITION. There was a bad case of child neglect at the Warring- ton Police Court on Monday, but taking all the circum- stances into consideration the magistrates felt justified in giving the defendant an opportunity of amendment. William Caldwell,labourer,Hamiltonrst was the defendant and he was charged with neglecting his four children in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering and injury to health.—Mr. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., and said Caldwell earned 18s. per week. He had a son who earned 15s. The chief complaint of the Society was in regard to the child Lucy, aged two years and eleven months. Instead of weighing 27Ibs. she weighed only 141bs. and when Dr. Peacocke saw her,he immediately ordered to be taken to the Workhouse Infirmary. The bed on .whieh the child lay was covered with maggots.—lnspector Hackett said he had had the case under observation since 1901. When he visited the house on the 6th inst., in company with Mr. Frank Aukland he found three of the children were well nourished but badly clad. Their bodies were filthy dirty and their heads were verminous. The youngest child was very much emaciated and filthy dirty. The bedrooms andbedding were in a filthy state and an old cradle in which the youngest child slept was alive with maggots.—Dr. Peacocke gave evidence as to the condition of the child Lucy and said his opinion was that it had been insufficiently fed. The bedrooms were unfit for habitation and the cradle in which the youngest child slept was "one seething mass of maggots."—Evidence was also given by Mr. Frank Aukland, and Mrs. Bridge, milk dealer.—De- fendant, who is a widower, said that he had had diffi- culty in getting a suitable person to look after the children since his wife's death. He promised to obtain a woman's services if the magistrates adjourned the case.—With this understanding the Bench adjourned the case for a fortnight.

A SATURDAY NIGHT SCMTE AT LATCHFORD. Yesterday at the Warrington Police Court, before Mr. H. Roberts and other magistrates, Patrick Kennedy, a farm labourer, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Wash-lane, Latchford, at 11.35 on Saturday night and with assaulting John William Smith, an insurance agent, residing at 53, Wash-lane.—Smith stated that at 11.10 on Saturday night he was in his house and heard a disturbance taking place out- side. On going into the lane he saw the prisoner Kennedy and another man fighting. Kennedy knocked his opponent to the ground and another Irish- man kicked him. Someone shouted "Fair play. Let him get up," and the Englishman was allowed to get up. The Irishmen had in the mean- time obtained sticks and the Englishman escaped. Another man named Tickle was then attacked and knocked down. Whilst he was on the ground Kennedy and the other Irishmen belaboured him with a stick and a belt. Witness interfered and defendant struck him in the chest and kicked him about the legs. P.C. Burton came upon the scene and defendant again assaulted witness in his presence. He would not swear that defendant was either drunk or sober.—P.C. Burton said defendant was drunk and on being taken into custody struck Smith in the chest and licked him an the legs. Witness was obliged to handcuff Kennedy.—Sergeant Smith and P.C. Stewart gave evidence that defendant was drunk when taken to the Police Station.—For the defence Mr. Browne said that for a number of Saturday nights a number of loafers had been in the habit of waylaying Irishmen at the corner of Wash-lane. About three weeks previously Tickle attacked Kennedy and Mr. Bennett,a shopkeeper, had to interfere. Kennedy came from Warrington on a tramcar and was seen by the conductor and driver to be perfectly sober. In Wash-lane he was met by Tickle and a man namedCaldwelliwho told him that he would have to fight them. The men pulled their jackets off and hadijita fight and ilKennedy also had to fight another man. Someone hit defendant on the head with a stick and that was why the onlookers shouted "Fair play." Smith came out and pinioned Kennedy until P.O. !Burton arrived.— After hearing evidence the magistrates considered that defendant had already received sufficient punishment and dismissed both charges.—John Caldwell, another of the parties concerned was bound over and ordered to pay the costs for fighting in Wash-lane on the night in question.—Patrick Kennedy was then charged with assaulting James Tickle, Frederick-street.—Mr. Steel appeared for the complainant who alleged that defendant struck him about the head and body with a stick.—Com- plainant denied being a loafer and said he had never waylaid the defendant.—The assault was denied, but the magistrates fined defendant 20s.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

Minor Cases.

WEDNESDAY.—Before_ Mr. Birettall .(in the chair),

Alderman Monks and Smethurst.

.41441., DESERTION OF FAMILY.—James Butler, an ex-soldier, was charged with running away from the borough of Warrington, leaving his wife and four children charge- able to the Warrington Union. He pleaded guilty. Mr. Shearer, relieving officer, said the defendant left his wife and family in February and enlisted in the Army, Since that time they had been chargeable, and the cost to the union had been £l3. During the greater part of the time that he had been away defendant had been earning money, but had not sent a penny to his family. The wife was now very ill in the workhouse hospital, On defendant paying a down and promising to pay the remainder of the money, the case was adjourned for a month.

ALTERATIONS TO LICENSED HOUSES.

Applications at Warrington.

It will be remembered that at the last Licensing ; Sessions the Warrington magistrates renewed the licences of a number of houses subject to certain structural alterations being carried out. The matter I was again introduced at the Police Court on Monday by Mr. A. Browne on behalf of Messrs. Greet:fall, Whitley and Co. He informed the !Bench that the alterations to the "Bird in hand," Bank-street, "Brewer's Arms," Winwick-street, and "Traveller's I Rest," Fennel-street, had been completed but at i some other houses the required improvements had not been carried out. As the magistrates made a stipulation that the alterations should be effected within six months he had to apply for an extension of time in a number of cases, through no fault of the com- pany but through the fault of different persons. In regard to the "Brickmaker's Arms," School Brow, the Bench suggested that the adjoining property should be purchased. The company had great difficulty in obtain- ing it, but ultimately succeeded, though not in time to carry out the alterations within Six months. It had also been found that the plans submitted in March could not very well be acted upon and they there- fore asked the Bench to approve fresh plans. , The effect of the new plans would be that three rooms would be provided instead of four, but would occupy exactly the same area as wasjoriginally mentioned. The object was to secure greater privacy for the tenant and his family, and the drinking rooms would be entirely separate from the domestic apartments. I The Chairman intimated that the Bench had already disoussed the plans in private. Continuing, Mr. Browne made similar applications in regard to the " Royal Oak Branch "and • Glassmaker's Arms "pointing out that the proposed alterations would give greater privacy to the tenants. In regard to thenew " Brooklands hotel," the tenant found that there was insufficient bedroom accommodation and the com- pany ask for.43ermission to provide two additional rooms 1 I Extension of time for alterations—he suggested until the Licensing Sessions in February—were asked for in respect of" The Gardener's Arms, " Adelphi Vaults," and Old house at home," in addition to the houses he had already mentioned. The magistrates having consultedprivately, the Chair- man (Mr. F. Monks), announced that they had granted an extension of six months in respect of The Gardener's Arms," Adelphi Vaults " and " Old house at home." The alterations to the "Royal Old Branch," and "Glassmaker's Arms" were also approved. In regard to the "Brook lands hotel "the Bench granted the application subject to the new rooms being kept strictly private. Mr. Browne said they would be used entirely for domestic purposes. The Chairman said the intention of the Bench was that the rooms should not be used as club roomsor any- thing of that description. It was suggested that the doors should be marked "private." The magistrates approved of the alterations to the "Brickmaker's Arms" subject to there being no clubroom. There was some discussion on the latter decision,Council- lorThornton stating that his intention was that the room should be entirely omitted from the plan. Dr. Fox: Is the room to be built as a bedroom or not at all? _ .

Councillor Thornton thought the room should not be constructed at all. . .

The Chairman: The decision of the Bench is that the alterations be agreed to, but there is to be no club-room.

NO MORE MUSIC LESSONS. Perfect Pianos for Poor People.

Piano playing machines will soon bring perfect music within the reach of all. These machines are not "organs," but play any tune on an ordinary piano. Competition will bring their prices so low that every householder will be able to afford one. Children will no longer need the drudgery of Music Lessons. Some music teachers say the result will be to kill their profession. However, it is quite possible that
