Music teachers will be ruined.

they should assist a teacher. For instance, Madame Lennox, the well-known teacher of music, at one time might have found the instrument of the greatest help. It would certainly have saved a little of the strength she had lost. But thanks to Dr. Williams' pink pills Madame Lennox is now quite able to play for herself. Interviewed by the "Manchester Weekly Chronicle" at her home at 2 Danny-street, Ardwick, Manchester, Madame Lennox said that the long and tiring journeys she had to take professionally were what told on her, and the time came when they quite pros- trated her.

"From a girl," she said, "I had suffered from anmmia. Music teaching is hard work, and ,not being strong I found it,too much for me. My nerves were shattered, and a short time ago I could scarcely crawl about, let alone give attention to my pupils and engagements. In London I was so terribly low-spirited and broken down that I went to stay at a farm in Derbyshire for twelve months." "Did that have any beneficial effect ?"

' No. When I went I was too weak to go upstairs, and had to sleep downstairs. I had fainting fits, and such attacks, of dizziness came over me that for weeks I was too weak to go over the farm. I went back to Loudon nearly ag bad as when I left, and then I found a cure in Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. The pills did me good almost from the first. They seemed to put new life into me. My appetite and spirits returned, the dizziness stopped, and colour came to my cheeks. Now lam quite well and able to go on with my musical duties without feeling any ill effects of the overstrain that is, .I fear, a part of a teacher's life."

Weariness and -disgust- viith one's work arises more often than not from bad health. The energy needful to enjoy work depends on sound nerves, a good supply of blood, anda perfect digestion. Dr. Williams'pink pills contain within themselves what, in the stomach, makes new blood. They send health coursing through every vein. Anzemia, the curse of young womanhood, cannot exist where they are taken. They do not tinker with disease; they remove the causes of it. Poor blood— blood that has become poisoned by fatigue and bad con- ditions of life—is at the root of such ailments as rheu- matism, scrofula, eczema and other skin diseases, gout. and the ailments which women do not care to talk about. That is why these Pills have proved so success- ful in the cure of these things. Of course it is the genuine pills that cure. There is no longer any need to warn sensible people that the substitutes which some shopkeepers try to sell in place of them never cured any- thing. If they had, the world would have heard of the fact: everyone has heard of Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people and the cures they have effected, and all buyers who have anyrespect for theirhealth and pocket now take care to see Dr. Williams' name on the package. Dr. Williams' medicine company, Holborn-viaduct, London, will send the pills post free for two and nine- pence—six boxes thirteen and ninepence—if there is any difficulty in getting the genuine, and will be glad to have the name and address of any tradesman who offers substitutes. Nervous ailments such as St. Vitus' dance, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, and neuralgia; and disturbances of the stomach like biliousness and indiges- tion, have been as promptly cured by these pills as the other diseases named.

Play as well as work needs good health for its enjoy- ment. ' Without it, the Piano playing machines, soon to be brought within the reach of all, could give as little pleasure as the Music Lessons they will supersede.

OLDEST ODDFELLOW DEAD.

An interesting figure in the social life of Derby passed away on Thursday in the person of Mr. John Hill who on February 28 last completed his hundredth year, and who possessed the distinction of being the oldest Oddfellow in the world. ' The old gentleman, who was a retired bookseller, was the recipient of many con- gratulations on his last birthday, which he oelebrated by distributing £l5O amongst various local charities. He became ill on Monday, and passed quietly away on Tuesday morning, the cause of death being senile decay. His claim to be the oldest Oddfellow in the world was well founded ;he was initiated in the Loyal Edwin Lodge at Alfreton in 1823, and on that lodge becoming defunct was transferred twenty years later to the Spring of Love Lodge at Derby. He was a teetotaller and non-smoker. Ile was an old-fashioned Radical, and up to the last never tired of discussing the horrors of the days of Protection.

The famous White Star liner Britannia went on her last voyage on Tuesday. At noon she was towed out of Belfast Harbour, where she had been lying since December, by the powerful Hamburg tug Roland, for Bremen, where she will be turned into "scrap." She was built in 1874.

The Examiner, Saturday, AuguA 15, 1903.

MUNICIPAL TRAD:':I

TOWN COUNCIL SANCTIONS AN

INQUIRY.

A Warning from Councillor

F. R. Roberts.

The Amendments Last.

The Warrington Town Council has at length de- cided to hold an inquiry into the trading transactions of the Corporation for the past seven years. The soheme drawn up by the joint committee of the Town Council and the Non-Trading representatives was on Tuesday evening accepted in its entirety. Councillor F. R. Roberts moved the amendments of which he had given notice and found a seconder in his brother, Councillor William Roberts. This was all the support the amendments received with .the exception of one relating to the inquiry being open to the public and the press. This was suPported by Alderman Bennett,

but was lost, only three voting for it. THE PROPOSED SCHEME. Councillor TINNION moved:—

(1) That an inquiry be held into all trading by past and present members of the Council or officials of the Corporation with the Corporation, either direct or indirect, through limited companies or officials of such companies, or as contractors or sub-contractors, or any dealings or transactions of any kind whatever between members of the Town Council or officials of the Corpora- tion and the Corporation. (2.) That the inquiry shall be made by six persons to c be alled Commissioners, three of whom shall be selected by the Town Council and three by the members of the Non-trading Committee. That each side shall agree to the persons selected without demur, and that neither side shall appoint a. lawyer. _ _

(3) Thaahe period Of inquiry shall be from the Ist of April, 1896, to the 31st March, 1903. (4) That the costs of the inquiry shall be borne by the Corporation, and that the Commissioners shall receive no remuneration.

(5) That the Commissioners shall have power to call anybody and everybody,whether a member of the Council official or servant, contractor, sub-contractor, or other, person, to give evidence or information touching the inquiry. (6) That the Commissioners shall have power to call for every minute book, and every book, paper, and document of the Council and of any and every com- mittee to be at the service of the Commissioners, or of such clerks or assistants as are acting for the Com- missioners.

(7) That any person who desires to give evidence (one way or the other) shall be allowed to do so, pro- vided that such evidenoe has, in the opinion of the Commissioners, a direct bearing upon the matter to be inquired into. (8) That the Mayor shall give a written indemnity to any witness, being an employs of the Corporation, and requiring it, in respect of any truthful evidence he may give. (9) That neither the public nor the Press be admitted •to the sittings of the Commissioners. (10) That a shorthand report of the proceedings shall be taken by such persons as the Commissioners may appoint. (11) That the Commissioners shall report their find- ings to the Town Council and the Non-trading Com- mittee.

In moving the adoption of the foregoing scheme of in- quiry which was recommended by the Joint Committee Councillor Tinnion pointed out that it originally stood in the name of Alderman Bolton, but the Alderman withdrew his motion as a protest against the action of the Council in deciding to adjourn the matter for a month in order that Councillor F. R. Roberts might

move some amendments. He (Councillor Tinnion) thought Alderman Bolton carried the protest too far and committed what he considered a breach of confidence between Councillor Evans aud himself (Councillor Tinnion). The reason he (Councillor Tinuion) adopted the resolution was because he felt that while the reso- lution was put down in the name of Alderman Bolton it was equally the resolution of Councillor James Evans and himself. Therefore he thought Alderman Bolton ought not to have given up the resolution in a fit of temper or disgust without consulting the other repte- sentatives of the Council on the Joint Committee. Continuing Councillor Roberts pointed out that any person signing a report alluding to any member of the Council in tams that were slanderous or of a derogatory nature was liable not only to proceedings for damages far any insult or injury that might reeult but he might also find himself landed into proceedings for criminal conspiracy. Let them distinctly understand what they were doing. The greatest objection he had to the whole of the resolutions was that although the resolutions provided that the Commissioners should have power to call anyone they liked, there was no pro- vision anywhere that anyone against whom charges had been made should have the right to attend and refute those charges, and there was no provisioa that they should be acquainted with the charges. There was no right given to a man to know what charges had been made against him. If that was so how could he come before a tribunal and answer them? In fact here they had a tribunal half composed of men who had made the charges, and they defied the right of every man under English law to know precisely the charge made against him; to be present when it was made; or•to give evidence to rebut the charges. If he might repeat the words of counsel whom he consulted: Are we back

again to the days of the inquisition; are we back again to the days of medieval times? Do these gentlemen think that English law will tolerate things of this kind?" He (Councillor F. R. Roberts) warned thore me mbers of the Council who were members of the Non- Trading Committee not to vote for these resolu- tions at all, because they were voting in the capacity of members of the Non-Trading Committee and members of that Council. He would also warn any gentleman who might be tempted to sit on this tribunal and who made charges, of the consequences that might ensue. To be forewarned was to be forearmed. He was content to leave his amendments, believing that after all there was in every Englishman an inherent love of fair play which would not allow anyone to be denounced, condemned and then tried by the very men who had made the charges against them. He could not think that would commend itself to the sense of justice of any man now that the unwisdom, unkindness and nn- righteousness had been put clearly before them. (Hear, hear.) GORNALL. Service at Bold-street Church.

The grave closed over the remains of the late All': Mary Gomall, widow of the late Dr. J. H. Gornall,. twenty-one years Medical Officer of Health for Warriag' ton, at the Warrington Cemetery on Saturday afterne(lt The cortege left the late residence of the deceased laul Ribblesdale, Grappenhall, at about one o'clock? 11°, proceeded to Bold-street Wesleyan Church, where short servite, conducted by the Revs. John Gunnell al" W. C. J. Nelville, was held. In the course of an addTe the Rev. J. Gunnell said it was very fitting that .t last public service, in connection with the life Jas; closed should be held in that building. It was thherr that she heard her first sermon, and sang, apart from It, own home, her first Christian hymn. She was broug.o there as a little child and was attached to the place the very end of her days. That church was, in s 11t2.% peculiar and special sense, her religions home. She enrolled into active church membership nearly bak, century ago, and her attachment for everything 0.100 nected with the church was very strong. Her afflict' vtas long and very painful, yet with what wonderfa courage she bore it! They would ever think of her,hl a true Christian, with a heart full of compassion for ;7;0 poor; a true friend; loyal to the church of her cao',;ci and of her youth; in full sympathy with every g,ter work; an affectionate sister, and -a devoted whose "children arise up and call her blessed." /dr The service at the graveside was impressivell cjo ducted by the Rev. _ Councillor THORNTON drew attention to the want of lighting from Orford-lane to the borough boundary, and mentioned that on Sunday night August 2nd there was net a single gas lamp lighted there at 9.30 p.m. It was a fearfully wet and dark night and such a night on which he expected that every lamp would be lighted. Alderman SUTTON pointed out that at the meeting of the Street Improvement and Lighting Committee the question was discussed and referred to the Lighting Sub-committee, with power to act in the matter and settle terms with the Gas Committee.

Councillor JAMES EVANS said it was a very difficult question to settle. The area of the town had so much increased, and what was at one time lit up in an hour and forty minutes took two hours. The Sub-Lighting Committee and Gas Committee had agreed that the men should start half-an-hour earlier, and they were putting on four extra men. The work was to be distributed so that a great deal of the difficulty complained of would _ _ .

THE PROPOSED BRANCH LIBRARIES. A NEW DIFFICULTY.

The Museum Committee submitted the following re- port :—" That the following report on the desirability of providing branch libraries be submitted to the Coun- cil. The comnittee have considered the question of the desirability of establishing branch libraries, and report that a branch library might take any of the fol- lowing forms :—(l). An independent lending library with separate staff and stock of books and with.a newsroom attached. (2) A newsroom with small reference library attached. (3) A delivery station with small stock of books on deposit. (4) A school issue centre for the use of scholars only. Having regard to the extent and distribution of the population of the borough, and to the question of expense, the committee do not suggest at present the formation of any independent branch, but consider that it is desirable to set up one or more delivery stations. These would be established, by preference in shops, the shopkeeper acting as agent, and being paid a certain sum per week for accommodation and attendance. A small selection of books would be deposited at each station, and, in addition, any book might be requisi- tioned by a borrower from the central library, and delivered to him at the station. The cost of such branches would depend partly upon the frequency withiwhich books were exchanged between the branch and the library. It may be roughly estimated at £2O a year for each station. It is not anticipated that any capital expenditure ...would be required. The committee recommend that, as an experiment, not more than four delivery stations be opened, and that the Council make a special grant for this purpose of £6O for the remainder of the current financial year. The committee further propose to take steps to form issue centres in elementary schools, with the help and under the control of the head teachers. This will not involve any appreciable increase of expense, except suchas may arise from increased issue. Alderman BENNETT, in moving the minutes,announced with regret the retirement of Alderman Monks from the Committee. During the time he had sat on that Com- mittee he had taken the greatest possible interest in its work and been of invaluable service. With regard to branch libraries, the sub-committee proposed to fall in with Councillor Upson's suggestions to some extent. They had decided to move cautiously, and were going to establish delivery stations for a commencement. The Tows CLERK pointed out that no special grant could be made for the purpose, as the Committee was not allowed to exceed a lid. rate.

Alderman BENNirr- : You can't make a special grant. The TOWN CLERK : No.

Alderman BENNEIT said they were anxious to give all the facilities they could, but if they were restricted in their resources they could not give those facilities. He was afraid that after what the Town Clerk had said the scheme must be withdrawn for further considera- tion.

Councillor ELLISON seconded. Councillor LoRD moved that the accounts of the Museum rommittee be referred back. The accounts included an item of a few shillings which had beenpaid for a circular issued by Mr. F. Monks advocating the claims of Mr. Madeley for an increase of salary. The circular was sent out from Mr. Monks's private address, and he thought the cost should be borne privately, and not by the Council. Alderman BENNETT explained that the -circular was issued on behalf of the committee and was only signed by. Mr. Monks as chairman. Councillor DODDS said he called attention to the matter in Committee, and he did not think it worth while doing more. The minutes were then confirmed With the exception of the branch library scheme. It was also resolved that Councillor Wilkinson be a member of this committee in place of the late Councillor Baird.

Councillor LORD proposed the adoptinn of the minutes of the Markets Committee.

Councillor ROGERS objected to the proposed removal of a stall in the Fish Market at the request of Messrs. Reardon Bros. and moved that it be referred back. Councillor JOHN ROBERTS seconded.

Councillor PEA.COCK explained that they were getting 2s. a week more and the alteration was also a public convenience.

Councillor HORROCKS supported the committee and pointed out that the fish stalls were very inconveniently constructed, and needed taking down and rebuilding. CARS FOR THE LABOURING CLASSES.

- - The Electricity and Tramways Committee resolved : --" That the Town Clerk be instructedto make an appli- cation to the Board of Trade for an extension of time for the construction of the tramways from the Cemetery to Padgate and Bruche Bridge ; and that the com- mittee meet on. Monday next at three o'clock to inspect proposed routes for additional tramways. A petition from the tramway employs for holidays was referred to the General Purposes Committee. On the 13th July the Committee met at the Town Hall and subsequently inspected Lovely-Lane, Bowsey-road, Tanners-lane, Orford-lane, Marshhousc!-lane, Pad- gate-lane, and King, Edward-street as suggested routes .for additional trainvvays.—At a meeting on the 22nd July, it was resolved that the tender of Messrs. Kelvin and James White, Limited, for a feeder panel for traction switchboard required for tramway purposes at £36 15s. be accepted. The ques- t:on of construction of additional tramways was, after discussion, deferred until June next.—At a meeting on August sth, the following resolutions were passed That the plans prepared by Messrs Preece and Cardew of the' proposed Light Railway No. 1 be approved, and submitted to the Cheshire County Council and the Stockton Heath Parish Council; and that the Property Purchasing Committee be requested to conduct the negotiations for the purchase of the premises required for the widening of Old London-road; that the tender of Mr. Thomas Stringer for the construction of the foundation bed for the new engine at £l5l 17s. 6d. be accepted, aud that Mt. Grimsdale be requested to make arrangements for the running of a service of cars for the labouring classes within the hours provided for in the Tramway Order.

In, moving the adoption of the minutes, Alderman MONKS referred to the question of depreciation. As it had never been brought forward there had been no need to consider it and therefore he had no opinion on the matter. Apart from that, it waif not a question of what he thought but of what the committee and the Council thought. When the Council had expressed an opinion he should carry it out loyally. The £9O profit on the tramway undertaking was transferred to a reserve fund which could be used for depreciation or anything else the Council saw fit.

Councillor DODDS asked how they would be able to deal with depreciation in case of loss?

Alderman BOLTON asked what was the use of provid- ing for depreciation when they had no money to do so. (Laughter.) If they had no profits they would have to levy a rate to provide the deprediation fund. He con- tended that the time to levy the rate would be when they required new machinery, &c. Councillor PEMBERTON said that every properly con- ducted business concern in the country that owned machinery had a depreciation fund. The proper course for the glectricity and Tramways Committee to adopt was to write off so much for depreciation. Ire was sorry that business men got up in the Council and made sue, assertions as Alderman Bolton's.

_ _ AI derman BOLTON: You don't know what you are talking about. (Laughter.) Councillor PEMBERTON contended that the tramway property had alreadvdepreciatedand that a depreciation fundfought to have -been opened at the outset. Of course nabody knew anything except "these old Parliamentary hands. (Laughter.) Continuing he referred to the tender of Messrs. Kelvin and White to supply a feeder panel for .£36 15s. and said he found Messrs. Preece and Cardew received commission on the order. When they had an electrical engineer reemiving a magnificent salary and he had to go to Messrs. PARLIAMENTARY COMMITTEE.

Councillor LORD asked the Mayor :—(1) Was the Town Clerk authorized by the Mayor, or any committee of this Council, to attend before tile Committee of Lords and Commons re "The question of municipal trading," to give evidence? (2) If not, on whose authority did he attend, and will the ratepayers be called upon to bear the expenses incurred? The MAXOR replied that the Town Clerk consulted him about going to London, and he asked Mr. Whittle if he wished to go at his own cost or at the cost of the Corporation. The Town Clerk most distinctly told him that he wished to go at his own cost. Therefore, there was no occasion for the matter to come before any com- mittee, and the only leave he (the Mayor) had to give him was leave of absence, which he did most willingly. (Hear, hear.)

MR. J. M. MURRAY'S SALARY. THE SECONDARY SCHOOL STAFF.

Councillor BENNETT moved the adoption of the minutes of the Education Committee. Councillor PEMBERTON referred to Mr. J. Moore Murray's salary. He contended that according to the resolution adopted by the Education Committee the in- crease should not have commenced until twelve months from the date on which it was agreed to. He moved that the accounts of the Education Committee be re- duced by the extra sum which the director had been re- ceiving. There was a strong feeling in the town in regard to the appointment of teachers for the secondary school, the salaries amounting to £l,OOO per annum. It was the opinion of most people that the Corporation were rushing on very much too quickly in regard to educational matters, when they might reasonably wait and take the example of Liverpool and other large towns which were not rushing on so quickly. Salaries to the amount of £l,OOO were very large •to teach only 100 persons. A great many people—and some members of the coMinitteehad spoken to him on the matter—would have liked the headmaster to have been appointed first, and then he could have advised the committee as to the staff he would requite. If they kept on expending money in this way the rates would soon be Bs. 4d. Before the Council was asked to confirm the committee's minutes the teachers were advertised for. That was done to a great extent in that Council In connection with other committees. In acase Like the present it was a rather serious matter as the charge 'would be an annual] one. The Council ought to have a real say in the business of the Corporation. It seemed to him there was too much power delegated to committees.

Councillor LORD seconded the amendment.

===== 0002601_19030815_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
AldermanßENNarr said his own impression was that the original intention of the committee was that the increase should not be paid for a year. When the second debate took place however, the committee came to the conclusion that the intention was that thesalary should commence at once. They decided under all the circumstances to waive the point and let the increase commence at once, and he thoughtthe decision was fair and reasonable. In regard to advertising he agreed with the principle as stated by Councillor Pemberton, but there were conceivable emergencies when no rule could be absolutely binding and a certain amount. of latitado had to be given to committees. The advertise- ment in question was issued after consideration and mainly with a view to seeing that the school should commence at the proper time. Unless this course had been adopted it would not be possible to make arrangements for the school to commence in autumn as they hoped it would. At first sight it appeared to him that the salaries were perhaps exhorbitant but no one seemed to understand at the outset that the staff in addition toteaching 100 scholars would have to teach 94 pupil teachers. They would also be expected to assist in the work of the Technical classes. 'The -CB7O was not the only sum they would have to pay in salaries. At present they were paying Mess Warburton and Mr. Buxton Lao each and this would make the total amonot £1,130. They would have to deduct £ll5 which was being paid to various teachers in connection with the Technical Institute alone and £5OO which .was expended in connection with the old Pupil teachers centre Councillor Bennett pointed out that the students at the secondary school would be educated atisemething like half the cost for teachers as compared with the Grammar school. They were doing the work economically if they were to have thoroughly efficient teachers and thoroughlyefficient education. Alderman MONKS said that since the proposal of the Committee was brought forward theßoard of Education had issued a memorandum giving details in regard to secondary schools. This would in effect make it com- pulsory for every education authority to support such a school as the Warrington committee was now support ing. Councillor BURTON referred in terms 'of praise to Mr. Murray's qualifications and supported the action of the committee.

Councillor DODDS said he should vote for the amend- ment because he disapproved of the irregular manner in which Mr. Murray was paid two or three months salary in face of the resolution which was quite clear on the point, that the increase should commence in twelve months.

The voting was then proceeded with and resulted as follpws:—

HOMING.

The Warringtoa and District Homing Society had their second young bird race, from Gloucester (107 miles) on Saturday. Mr. S. Taylor (convoyer) liberated the birds at Gloucester, at 11.30 a.m. in fine weather, and with a south wind. Mr. P. Leicester, Stockton Heath, timed the first bird in at 1.10 p.m., being one ,hourand 40 minutes for the journey, or an average velocity of 1,815 yards per minute. Twenty-six mem- bers sent 325 birds. The Examiner, Saturday August 15, 1903. WARRINGTON SHOW. THURSDAY'S SUCCESSFUL EXHIBI- TION AT WALTON.

imes traceable to the stupid bigotry of individual parsons, but not infrequently the consequence of a growing sense of the grave doctrinal differences between the teaching of the Church and the convic- tions of the people. But, on the whole, Disestablishment cannot, I think, be considered as yet a popular demand. What of our statesmen and leaders? How are they disposed to the question? This is not the heroic age of statesmen. Hr. Balfour is not the only promi- Inent politician who has not settled convictions. In 1885 Mr. Chamberlain used to explain his animosity against the Establishment by referring to ancestors of his own who, so he said, were" ejected" from their livings in 1662, having, no doubt—though Mr. Cham- berlain never referred to this—" intruded" themselves upon previous occupants in Cromwellian times. None of our present Liberal leaders are in the least likely to produce " ejeoted " ancestors to give colour to their speeches on this topic. They are men of milder mood, and for the most part conformists to the rites and ceremonies of the Estaly, lishment. Besides from a House of Commons point of view—Disestablishment and partial Disendow- ment would be a gigantic undertaking. Obstruction would raise its head unabashed. The guillotine would never cease falling. The "wear and tear " would be stupendous. It would be easier to abolish the House of Lords. Clearly, in an unheroic age, Disestablish- meat is not an attractive policy. Another noticeable feature of our time is ' grow- ing love for pageants and public show. The very ilawyers have taken to going to Westminster Abbey on the first day of Michaelmas term. It is not a par- ticularly pious or impressive ceremony—or "function," if that be the right word—but it is symptomatic of much. The Coronation was a great spectacle. If the Church is disestablished, who is to crowa the next King? What religion is he to profess? May he be a Baptist, and if a Baptist, why not a Paptist ? If the Empire is ever to be federated, decency demands that the event should be celebrat4 in some building more associated with deep emotion than the Mansion House. Westminster Abbey and St. Paul's Cathedral alone answer the needs of national life when touched with. feeling; and without Establishment who would con- duct the services?

These may not be important or relevant questions, but they are in the air just now, and would be asked and answered were the controversy to become active.

As things are at present, I do wit think there is any chance of Disestablishment and Disendowment entering the arena of practical politics for years to come ; but of this lam even more sure—that the whole question will bej increasingly discussed, and with growing fervour, day by day. The first step taken will, and must be, the modification of the Education Act of last year in well known respects ' • and when this has been done the ultimate fate of the Establishment will prob- ably be found to depend upon the strength or weakness of the Protestantism of the nation. A Protestant Establishment may long endure. The present status quo cannot. It is dishonest, dangerous, and absurd.

[This article forms one of a series which we have arranged with:leading members of the Eighty Club to contribute to:our columns. Book copyright strictly re- served. The next article will be by Mr. George Greenwood, entitled "The House of Lords."]

Usually so quiet and peaceful, the village of Walton was all bustle and animation on Thursday when what is now generally known as the " Warrington Show" was held. It is not every agricultural society that. can boast of such a picturesquely-situated show ground as Walton Park, but thanks to the generosity of its esteemed President, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., the Warring 4:)n and District Agricultural Society annually avails itself of the privilege so readily conceded of holding its show within the borders of Sir Gilbert's beautiful detne,sne. Surrounded on all sides by charm- ing cnintry, Walton Park is - admirably adapted for show purposes, but many • townspeople who would not lay claim to a knowledge of agri- culture or kindred pursuits . take advantage of the opportunity of spending a pleasant time in beautiful Cheshire by visiting the show Sir Gilbert always throws the trimly-kept grounds,stables, piggeries, &c., open to the inspection of the visitors and the concession is highly appreciated. The officials of the Society have every reason to congratulate them- selves upon the signal success which attended Thurs- day's show. For once the elements were in a cheerful mood. Brilliant weather favoured the opening of the show" and with the exception of showers late in the afternoon the conditions throughout the day were ideal.l The result was to be seen in the large concourse of visitors. Vehicles of all descriptions streamed up to , the park and in the afternoon the ground presented an appearance of brightness and animation. No fewer than 3,000 persons paid for admission during the day, and in addition the members, exhibitors, and attendants present would number about 1,000. The attendance would be about double that. of last year. Of the show itself, there was a unanimity of opinion that it was a credit to the society. Several new features were introduced. Mr. J. H. Stokes,of Market Harborough, who for some years has been a very successful ex- hibitor, offered several. prizes, and this enabled the society to form an additional hunter class. There were also two classes for polo ponies and they attracted a fair number of exhibitors, considering that they were an entirely new departure. The entries, in most departments showed an improvement upon last year, but there was a falling off in the shire classes. It was also regrettable that there should be a marked decrease in the sections for vanners' or light lurry horses, and heavy horses with vehicles. In the last named class, instead of the heavy entry which is usually experienced, the exhibits which entered the ring numbered only three, excluding two sent by Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Limited, which were not for competition. In the cattle classes there was a gratifying improvement, but the pig classes were not by any means as well filled as might reasonably have been expected. FEATURES OF THE SHOW.

The Warrington showholds a high reputation for the quality of the shire horse exhibits, which was fully maintained. Mr. James Gould, of Lyman, was as usual to the fore with animals of superior quality. His splen- did Lyn= Bounce—a big-boned, active typical shire— headed the district mare class. The barren mares were another excellent class, and Mr. Gould again wen with a daughter of Carbon, eight years old. Yearling colts or geldings were a rather poor class, but fillies were a good lot. The best class in the section, however, was that for two-year-old fillies. Mr. Cavendish's Tatton Maggie—a gix-year-old bay with great girth, good feet, and splendid reach—took chief honours for mares in the open section, and was shown in bloom. Mr. Lewis Dodd's young mare, by Seldom Seen, was a good second. There was an exceptionally fine class of hackneys. Colonel Lees Knowles's Mystery ran Rosemary, the famous winner, very close. Hunters were an excellent entry in both numbers and quality. Being just before the season some of the best-known animals in the coun- try were here. Sir Gilbert Greenall offered special premiums for tenant farmers. The saddle classes of hunters were better than at many county shows. The winning four-year-old, Android, is the picture of a hunter, and has good riding shoulders. One of the best classes in the show was that for hunters foaled in 1896 or since, and up to 13 stone and not more than 14st. 71b. The judges were a long time deciding the places below the first. A very well shaped bay and an easy galloper of Mr. Drage's was first. The jumping contest was one of the most interesting features of the show and so keen was the competition that the first and second prizes were divided. The cattle, though not over numerous, were of an ex- tremely useful type for dairy purposes, and especially suitable for agricultural districts in thenear vicinity of large towns. Mr. S. W. Gould, of Lymm was a pro- minent winner. The two-year-old heifer class might, perhaps, have shown improvement, though the beast that gained premier honours was a fine specimen. Per- haps the best class was that for young bulls. There were only 13 entries in the classes for pigs, but the quality left little to be desired. For breeding sows, the winner (owner, Mr. John.Thontag, of Grappenhall) was a nice type of animal. The second winner fell off in certain points while the third was not developed but will probably make an even better sow than the one which was awarded the second prize. In class 46 for the best cottager's pig, the first prize winner was a fine specimen with very symmetrical proportions. In class 47, for the best gilt (any breed born in 1903), Mr. R. T. Bridge carried off first, second, and third prizes. The only other exhibitor was awarded the reserve prize for a very good animal. The work of judging was ably carried out by the following :—Shires and Draught Geldings: A. W. Richman and George Ball. Hunters: T. Wickham Boyntqii and the Earl of Orkney. Polo Ponies: Major- Egerton Green. Hackneys and Harness: C. W. Wilson and A. E. Evans. Jumpers: J. Hepherd and T. Barton. Pigs: J. Ashworth. Butter and Cheese: T. Welsby. Shoeing Examination: T. Hopkin and A. Munro. The exhibits at the private stands were very credit- able the following firms being represented :—Messrs. Garton's, Limited, seed growers and merchants, War- rington; Dickson and Robinson, seedsmen' Manchester; Dutton and Bean, agricultural implement agents, War- rington; Day and Sons, animal medicines, Crewe; Mrs. Massey, Acton Grange nurseries, near Warring- ton; Clibrans, nurserymen, Altrincham, Cheshire (exhibit of cut flowers) : Joseph Crosfield and Sons. Ltd., Warrington, agricultural lime and egg pre- serving solution; E. Parkinson, 19,Bridge-street; Earles- town (exhibit of horse condiment) ; Henry and Julius Cmsar, Knutsford (exhibit of rustic summer houses) ; the Stockton Heath Forge Company, 'Warrington (ex- hibit of agricultural implements) ; Joseph Muskett, wheelwright, Higher Walton, near Warrington (exhibit of carriages, Ste.) ; and Charles Kinns, nurseryman and seedsman, St. Helens. The display of cut flowers was a excellent one. During the day the well-known Kingston Mills Band played a capital programme in a manner which called forth favourable comments on all hands.

Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall as usual took a great interest in the show and did their utmost to make it a success. It is interesting to know that the receipts from the grand stand, which was erected qn the spot usually occupied by Sir Gilbert's private stand, were double theamount received from the same source last year. Praise is due to the whole of the officials for the expeditious way in which the arrangements were carried out, and especially to Mr. H. Beresford Melville who discharged the secretarial duties with ability.

live to see the members of their family grow up, each fulfilling a useful place in society and in the world at large. The Agricultural Society was doing a very great amount of good. He could remember not many years ago when a horse took first prize which would simply have not been looked at to-day. That showed perfectly well that those who used horses—and there were a great many represented that day—were getting a little pride and commonsense instilled into them in the direction of believing that the best article was the cheapest in the end. He hoped that spirit would be further cultivated, that the Society had many years of extreme usefulness before it, and that they all might live to see the time when they might congratulate the worthy President and his wife upon being grandparents. (Applause). Sir GILBERT GREENA.LLy in reply, expressed what great pleasure it afforded him to meet all his friends of the showing world and he hoped and trusted that with the best of luck they might be spared another year to meet at the Walton show. (Applause.)"

THE WRITER OF OUR NEXT "SPECIAL."

Mr. George Greenwood, the writer of the next article in our series of Political "Specials," was one of the original members of the Eighty Club. He was educated at Eton, where he was a contemporary of Mr. Herbert Gladstone, and then graduated at Cambridge. He first came into active political life during the great campaign against the Bulgarian atrocities, and has since then always belonged to the advance guard of the Liberal party. In the great struggle for Home Rule, Mr. Green- wood enthusiastically espoused the cause of the Irish, and during the critical period of the summer of 1886 was selected to second the vote of thanks to Mr. John Morley, M.P., at the great Eighty Club dinner of that year. At the historic meeting of the Club in the following year, when the split between the Dissentient Liberals and the Home Rulers became complete, Mr. Greenwood acted as teller, and received the visiting- cards of the defeated section as they filed out of the large dining-room of the Westminster Palace Hotel in token of resignation. In 1889, Mr. Greenwood made a gallant attempt to win a Unionist seat at Peterborough at very short notice; and last year the Liberal Association of that city unanimously re-invited him to fight their battle again at the next opportunity. Besides writing for some of the great reviews, Mr. Greenwood has the pleasant gift of the rhymester, and some delightful verses to Mr. Chamberlain from his pen appear in the current number of the Ele:tor, ending—

But now you're in for Zollverein, Protectionist—What ho! You're Mr. Seddon—Armageddon, Preferential Joe ! MUNICIPAL BO Wurlti GREENS. What the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee are Doing.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council held in Bold-street Wesleyan School on Tuesday evening under the presidency of Mr. J. Edwardson, the Secretary (Mr. F. Stott) reported that acting upon the instructions of the Council he wrote to the Town Clerk asking what was being done by the Town Council in the diretion of providing municipal bowling greens. He received a reply porn Mr. Whittle to the effect that It was originally the intention of the Town Hall, Parks. and Baths Committee to include a sum in this year's estimate for the provision of a bowling green, but owing to the large increase in the rates necessitated by the Education Act the item had been omitted. He (Mr. Stott) again wrote asking if from that letter the Council were to understand that nothing further would be done by the Town Council until a sum had been included in the es- timates for the provision of bowling greens. To this, the Town Clerk replied that he woul lay the letter be- fore the committee and he could not tell what the in- tentions of the members were until they had considered the letter. He (Mr. Stott) wrote asking for further particulars, and the Town Clerk replied stating that he was instructed by the committee to inform him that they had given instructions to the Borough Surveyor to prepare a plan and estimate for the provision of a bowling green, and that the committee had under con- sideration the question of providing a green within the borough. The matter would be reported in due course to the Council. _ _ .

Several members expressed satisfactionat the progregs which was being made.

THE LUNCHEON.

- As usual the President entertained a large company at luncheon, and afterwards the usual toaxts were honoured Captain Withington proposed" The Warrington and District Agricultural society "coupling with it the name of the president. He said he had been present at the meetings of the society from its inception and the shows had, with varied weather, been attended with the best of success.

In responding, Sir Gilbert Greene.ll said it had been a great pleasure and honour to him to have been president of the society since its foundation. They had not been very fortunate as regarded the weather in former years, in fact this was the first time the showhad been favoured with fine weather since it had been held .on the new ground. He thought they had every reason to con- gratulate themselves upon the very fine exhibition that day. Although the exhibits might have been more numerous he did not think they could be beaten in the matter of quality. If they got the best specimens of each breed for which they had classes he did not think they could ask for very much more. He thought a little more interest might be taken in the work of the Society by membars who lived in the neighbourhood, not only in regard to the show, but by trying to increase the membership. Everybody who had anything to do with shows and showing must know that the greater the number of people they had inter- ested in the undertaking, the greater its success would be. (Applause.) Mr. J. W. KENWORTH v proposed the health of "The Judges" and spoke of the confidence reposed in those gentlemen by the exhibitors. They looked upon the Warrington show as being the show which put ther right men in theright place,and they hoped the Society might prosper. (Applause.) Mr. C. W. WILSON responded. He had, he said,atten- ded the show for several years, and if the Chairman invited him to attend in future—whether as an official or as a guest—he should certainly come as long as he could possibly "wag a leg." (Izitiierter.) In regard to the hackney classes which he had judged there was cer- tainly a great improvement. (Applause.) Mr. H. ROBERTS proposed the toast of "The Presi- dent," and after facetiously remarking that Captain Withington had stolen his toast, said he thought he could forgivejhim for the reason that it was extremely diffi- cult to think of the Warrington and District Agricul- tural Society without at the same time thinking of its worthy President. (Applause), He could not separate from the toast of the President the name of his excellent wije. They all congratulated Sir Gilbert upon his choice. He had now added to himself a family, and they hoped that Sir Qilbert and Lady Greenall might

NOTICJi TO CORRESPONDINT.

Authors of letters intended for publication must mall cases send their names and addresses to the 'Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side ,only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

Correspondents are strongly advised to write ,briefly A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstancesundertake return themanuscripts which are not used.

, August 12th, 1903. Stockton Heath School Accommodation.

Sir,—The correspondence which hs arisen since the meeting of the Stockton Heath parishioners, is not only a sign of the interest which such meeting aroused but is also a healthy symptom of the mental awakening of some of the residents to the true position of affairs. Probably the last report of the Inspector which the School Managers have been good and wise enough to have had published will not only awaken a few others, but cause others to further refrain from the foolish habit of the ostrich, which buries its head in the, sand and thus is prevented from seeing that which is patent to everyone else around. The following is an extract from the Inspector's re- port :—"Boys' school—The staff is too small and the school is overcrowded." Surely further comment should be needless !—Yours faithfully, E. ENGLAND.

===== 0002601_19030815_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
The Non-Trading Movement.

Sir,—l have not hitherto taken any part for or against this agitation, but having watched it through all its phases from the beginning until. now I feel constrained to express my astonishment at the remarkable progress it has accomplished. At the outset many people prophesied its sudden and ignominious collapse..when the excitement of the great meetings had died away. But the present state of the movement proves that the Seers lamentably failed in their forecast. In spite of the open and determined hostility of an overwhelmingmajority of the Town Council and the secret machinations of the political clubs it has gone on securing triumph after triumph until now, when the whole town seems to have imbibed the non-trading spirit. This is a marvellous phenomenon in a constituency Where Tory and Radical cliques have so long been permitted to manipulate municipal affairs at their Own sweet will, and it augurs well for the future banishment of party politics from the Council chamber.

AM what is it all about? The movement is simply a power ul and concentrated exp Session of the fiat of the heavily burdened taxpayers that " axe grinding " in the Town Council of Warrington must come to an ead at once and for ever. Whatever the legal aspect-of this peculiar and unsavoury form of commission and tip-sharing may be, there is a strong taint of immorality about it which must be obvious to observers. Our local Press and mural literature at election times abundantly confirm this. For then it is seen by their speeches that the seekers of office for Corporation crumbs would have us believe that they are the purest and most disinter- ested of men. Indeed they go so far as to admit the well understood principle that the only reward due to a faithful Councillor for his public services lies in the approval of his fellow citizens and the satisfaction of his own conscience. In practice, however, they desire to supplement their reward by trading with the Corpor- ation for their own advantage. Is not the labourer worthy of his hire? And unfortu- nately, through the laxity of the ratepayers many of these selfish and doublefaced candidates are allowed to slip into office. There are, no doubt, honourable men in our local Parliament who would scorn to debase themselves and their constituents by stooping to such meanness and hypocrisy as this; but what are they among so many! As a matter of fact their influence -upon the Corporation as a whole is barely perceptible, contracting to a large extent having gone on for years without lot or hindrance.

Should the Non-Traders succeed In caryiing out the reform upon which they are bent the change would, .doubtless be greatly to the advantage of the Borough, lor in that case the foul blot of contracting would dis- Appear from the Council; extravagance would give place to strict economy in all departments; a healthy feeling of mutual confidence would spring up between the members of the Council and their constituents, and the permanent officials would assume less airs than they do now, and be relegated to their proper position as hired ..servants of the people.—Yours truly, •lONA. August, 1903.

The firm hoped shortly to put up still larger settling accommodation to take out, all solid matter from the effluent from the tannery. LONGFORD DEPOT.

THE DISESTABLISHMENT OF THE CHURCH.

BY AUGUSTINE BIRRELL, K.C. (President of the National Liberal Federation).

Eighteen years ago Mr. Chamberlain traversc.d the country, from Hackney to Inverness, advocating a Radical Programme which remains unfulfilled to this day. As a legislator the Colonial Secretary is not a success. In 1884 he was very confident about two things. " Protection," he told Birmingham," will lessen the total production of the country, will diminish the rate of wages, and raise the price of every necessary of life." When he got to Glasgow he told a huge audience that for political as well as for social reasons, and in the interest of religion itself, he was a Liberationist. " I would free the Church from State control whether in England, in Scotland, or in Wales." In 1903 Mr. Chamberlain is contemplating another campaign, in which he will assure the country that a measure of Protection will raise the rate of wages, and (if he is asked the ques- tion) that it does not matter a farthing whether the Church is .disestablished or not.lt is uotgiven to many men so completely to turn their back upon their former selves ; but it cannot be denied that Mr. Chamberlain's recantations lack originality, and do but give promi- neat expression to a change both of positive opinions and as to the relative importance of particular reforms which many men have experienced no less than he Although nobody in 1885 was likely to suggest that Mr. Chamberlain would live to advocate taxes on food, it was plain enough even then that the Protectionist heresy was deep-rooted in many hearts, and only 'lacked some decent pretext to lift its head once more in the market-place ; whilst the careful student of the ever-shifting scene of ecclesiastical strife could not havelailed to observe that the cry for Disestablish- ment was one which 'had grown fainter in volume as the years went by. - What is called the Case of Disestablishment has lost none of its force, and may, I think, quite fairly be said to grow stronger year by year. The Church.of England has not only long ceased to be co-extensive with the nation, but what may be called the Church of England View of Religion, the dominant view as entertained by the majority of devout Anglicans, has become more and more repulsive to the 4,oTeat body of Dissenters. The differences between the Bishop of London and Mr. Silvester Horne, for example, are more substantial and separative than those between the Bishop of London and any English prelate of the Roman Catholic Church. It is absurd to minimise these differences. The late Cardinal Manning and the late Mr. Spurgeon were both Christians, holding in a common unity of faith the great doctrines of the Trinity, of the Divinity of Christ, of the Atonement, of the Fall of Man, of Original Sin, and the Eternity of Future Punishment; and yet it would have been impossible for these two men to work together for five minutes on any common basis of Christianity, so vital and pervading was the -difference between them on the subject of the priesthood. At the present moment the gulf between Church and Dissent is broader and deeper than it has ever been in our history ; and it is more than ever impos- sible to pretend that the Church of England in any way represents the general religious sense of the com- munity. It does not do so now, and every year the hostility, based as it is, and will be, on an irreconcil- able difference incapable of being compromised, will grow fiercer. ' There is no a priori justification for a Church Estab- lishment which, so far from including the nation, affronts and deeply wounds the settled religious con- victions of probably one-half of that portion of the :population which has religiaus convictions aCall. The question is now much more of a theological -one than it used to be. When a Nonconformist sees -exposed for sale in a stationer's shop photographs of the :Bishops of what he is invited to consider his ,national Church bedizened with the significant because symbolical millinery of a sacrificing priesthood, he feels that the question of the Establishment is no longer one of expediency, but of right and wrong. Doctrine can no longer be kept out of the account— and the main objection of the Dissenter to the Established Church has become no less serious than this, because it teaches both in the Church and the rate-supported school-house, in the name of the State to which all belong, what the Dissenter believes to be eternali falsehood. I need not stop to point out how the Education let of last year has accentuated the theologiCal side of this -embittered controversy. No living Nonconformist can remember a time when feeling ran as high as it does to-day. Disestablishment, though it would not, of course, touch those root-differences of opinion which of necessity create the cleavage, would—so it is be- lieved—by creating equality remove the particular bitterness now imported into the situation. So far, then, as Nonconformists are concerned, they are more than ever what Mr. Chamberlain was in 1885—Liberationists. I can remember when many Nonconformists of a devout turn of mind were luke- warm about Disestablishment, and fought shy of the Liberation Society. I know no such men now. The issue has become too clear and the stakes too high to admit of any such frame of mind. But what is the attitude of Churchmen? Here I tread unfamiliar ground, and speak hesitatingly. Years ago there was certainly a goodly number of High Churchmen who favoured Disestablishment as the price of freedom from the secular arm and the horrors of the conti d'dire. Their numbers have, I think, been reduced, Tor the good Anglican-7-as he now loves to call himself—hugs the delusion, as I must think it, that some day Lord Hugh Cecil will be able to persuade the House of Commons to give the Church of England complete ecclesiastical freedom, whilst permitting her to retain her political union with the State. It must never be forgotten that a man who feels his salvation to depend upon his belong- ing to a branch of the Catholic Church will be slow to accelerate the-hour when his branch may be lopped into three. Such a man prefers to preserve "unity," even by an Act of Parliament than not at all. The old 'Broad Church party died :in the cold. Nothing ever came its way. It was always against Disestablishment. A new Broad Church party is slowly appearing above the horizon, which aims at comprehension of all the fairly orthodox Churches; A. vain dream, I am persuaded; but this new party is also against Disestablishment. The Evangelical party in the Church- of England is by no means so moribund as .some fine folk suppose though no doubt it is always harassed by an illo,gidal position. But behind it there is the vast, latent 'Protestantism of the nation, to which if it ever chose to appeal in trumpet tones it would not appeal in vain. ITo the Evangelical Disestablishment has always been the last resort. He does not dread it so much as his ' High Church brother, because he is not frightened Ito death at sChisna ; but he has no mind for it, still believing, as he does that in the long run the Protestantism of the does, of England will drive out Lord Halifax and his friends, and render possible a

===== 0002601_19030815_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
Pearson and Knowles's Team Defeated in the Final.

After a series of keenly contested matches the War- rington Works Bowling Competition ended on Saturday afternoon in a victory for the London and North-Western Railway team who in the final were pitted against Messrs. Pearson and Knowles's No. 1 team. Interest in the competition has been thoroughly maintained from the commencement, no fewer than 20 teams having given evidence of their skill in the game. The result of the final came as asurprise to many enthusiasts the forge men being generally expected te carry off first honours. By the small margin of six points the railway team proved victorious and are to be congratulated upon their performance. A handsome silver cup presented by Mr. J. Finlayson is the trophy of which the winning team became possessors. It is beautifully designed and is surmounted with the figure of a bowler in the act of delivering his bowl. There was a large attendance of spectators who had the satisfaction of witnessing some capital play. The following were the scores :

PEARSON AND kNOWLES. L. AND N.-W. RAILWAY. J. W. Worsley l5 J. T Whittle W. Mee lO W. Bridgin J. Jennings J. Worsley T. Davies J. Hardman C. Littlewood l3 R. Mercer l5 H. Williams l3 J. Hutchinson l5 E. Gibson 9 J. Millington P. Ball J. Gleave George Ball (capt.)

The cup and medals—handsome gold ones presented by Mr. F. Clare for the winners, and silver ) nes with enamelled centres for the runners-up—are to be presen- ted later in the season at a smoking concert. After the match _several speeches were made. _

Mr. WILLErr-said this was the first time a works bowling competition had been ventured upon in War- rington, and he could assure them that the officials were quite satisfied with the conduct shown by the players all along. He had never seen anything carried on with such order, and they felt proud that the competitionhad been so successful. He confessed to feeling a bit sorry for their friends, the Pearson and Knowles team, but he could only say: "Better luck next time." (Hear. hear.") The association now intended to promote a handicap, which would be confined to the works whose clubs were affiliated with them. A friend had offered to give -£lO towards prizes— (applause)—and the entries would close on the 18th inst. He thought they could show their employers that they could come out there on a Saturday afternoon and enjoy -their game of bowls without getting drunk. (Hear, hear.) Of all the games he had seen he thought there had been less drunkenness in connection with that competition than he had seen with anything. He was sorry their President (Captain G. R. Crosfield) was not there—he was training his men to fight for King and country in the Isle of Man—but he would ask Mr. Taylor to say a few words. Mr. TAYLOR complimented the association on the manner in whioh the competition had been carried through, and also complimented both teams who had played. He always liked to see the best men win, and the best men had undoubtedly won that day taking the play in bulk. He could only say to the losing team: "Buck up! there is success awaiting you." (Applause.) Councillor HULSE, said the game was a national one, and had existed for hundreds of years in this country. He sincerely hoped England would continui to play the game, for not only was it a healthy one, but it did not tend to drunkenness, (Hear, hear.) It tended to keep men happy and also to keep good" homes. He congratu- lated both teams and honed the losers would sometime have the pleasure of beating the railway men. (Ap- plause.) The CHAIRMAN proposed a cordial vote of thanks to Mr. F. Clare, lamdlord of the King and Queen, for the way in which he had entertained them during the com- petition, and remarked upon the great pleasure it was to have among them that day such an old champion bowler as Mr. E. Knowles.

Mr. G. BALL seconded, and said that although he was on the losing side that afternoon, he had thoroughly enjoyed the game. Mr. T. HORRABIN, speaking on behalf of the winning team, congratulated his men upon the way in which they had played. Cordial votes of thanks were passed to the Chairman (Mr. Willett) and Secretary, (Mr. Scarisbrick.) Great credit is due to the Committee which had the arrangements in hand and especially to the capable secretary, Mr. Scarisbrick.

_ READY FOR WORK IN TWELVE DAYS AFTAR NINE MONTHS' SUFFERING. Farfeld-street, Bradford, Cleckbeaton, February 25th, 1903. Dear Sir,—l am firmly convinced that there ig nothing to equal your Magnetaire treatment for Rheumatism and Sciatica. I was off work Oho months suffering great pain with my back, hip and leg, and part of the time unable to sit in a chair. Seeing a testimonial you have received from the President of the Dewsbury Co-on.. I determined to try it, and to my great surprise I was able to declare off the club in twelve days and return to work. It was your appliances and nothing else that cleared the Rheumatism out of my system. There is one thing more that I should like you to know. I am fond of cycling, and during the summer heliday I made a big journey to Newcastle, cycling the whole distance, over one hundred miles, in a day. Wishing you every success.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON. TWENTIETH APPEARANCE. At the Newton-le-Willows Police Court, before Mr. T. J. Gillespie, Sarah Hickey, of Golborne, made her twentieth appearance for drunkenness, the fifth time in a few weeks. She was fined 10s. and costs; in default fourteen days. A VIOLENT EARLESTOWN MAN. OnMonclay Bernard Cahill, of Earlestown,was charged before Mr. Gillespieat Newton-le-Willows Police Court with being drunk and fdisorderly, and with assaulting Police-constable Mellor on Saturday night. When Cahill was apprehended he became so violent that it required several police officers to get him to the station during which Police-constable Mellor was kicked in the ribs. He was remanded until Thursday's sessions when he was fined 5s and costs for drunkenness and lOs and costs for assault. STREET BETTING AT EARLESTOWN. Another batch of Earlestown bookmakers, who last month were fined £5 and costs for betting, were again before the Newton-le-Willows magistrates on Thurs- day. In two cases Matthew Seaton and William Unsworth, of Earlestown, were each fined the full penalty in both cases. Frank Dale, also of Earlestown, was fined £5 and clsts in one case. John Allen, of Ashton-in-Makerfield, for bookmstking at Golborne, was also mulcted in £5 and costs; whilst Roger Kelly, William Henry Owen, and Alfred Pearce were fined 10s. and costs for betting with Allen. STONE-THROWING AT HANDOCK. Mr. E. Peace, in prosecuting a boy named Henry Brown, of Haydock, at Newton Police Court on Thurs- day, for throwing stones at a locomotive driver named , Sham, employed at Haydock colleries, stated that stone-throwing at Haydock had become so prevalent that special steps had to be taken to put a stop to the practice. Defendant was ordered to receive six strokes with the birch. AN EARLESTOWN DOG FIGHT. William Fahey and John Horan, an old man, were charged before the Newton-le-Willows magistrates on Thursday with cruelty to a dog at Earlestown on 3rd July. Several female witnesses, who appeared for the prosecution, stated that they heard Fahey say to one of the two dogs," Give it him, Jack," and when the dogs were fighting the two men refused to separate them. In cross-examination the witnesses admitted that they did not see either defendant hold one dog in such a manner as to cause it to attack the other. Inspector Print stated that the weaker dog had been bitten on the ears and fore leg. The Chairman said there was no proof that the defendants caused the dogs to fight, and dismissed the case.
